Sampling and Integration of Logconcave Functions by Algorithmic Diffusion
Yunbum Kook, Santosh S. Vempala

Abstract
We study the complexity of sampling, rounding, and integrating arbitrary logconcave functions. Our new approach provides the first complexity improvements in nearly two decades for general logconcave functions for all three problems, and matches the best-known complexities for the special case of uniform distributions on convex bodies. For the sampling problem, our output guarantees are significantly stronger than previously known, and lead to a streamlined analysis of statistical estimation based on dependent random samples.



1
Introduction

Sampling and integration of logconcave functions are fundamental problems
with numerous applications and important special cases such as uniform
distributions over convex bodies and strongly logconcave densities.
The study of these problems has led to many useful techniques. Both
mathematically and algorithmically, general logconcave functions typically
provide the “right” general abstraction. For example, many classical
inequalities for convex bodies have natural extensions to logconcave
functions (e.g., Grünbaum’s theorem, Brunn-Minkowski and Prékopa-Leindler,
isotropic constant, etc.). The KLS hyperplane conjecture, first motivated
by the analysis of the
\mathsf{Ball\ Walk}
for sampling convex bodies, is in the
setting of general logconcave densities. The current fastest algorithm
for estimating the volume of a convex body crucially uses sampling
from a sequence of logconcave densities, which is provably more efficient
than using a sequence of uniform distributions. Sampling logconcave
densities has many other applications as well, such as portfolio optimization,
simulated annealing, Bayesian inference, differential privacy etc.

Sampling in high dimension is done by randomized algorithms based
on Markov chains. These chains are set up to have a desired stationary
distribution, which is relatively easy to ensure. For example, to
sample uniformly, it suffices that the Markov chain is symmetric.
Generally, to sample proportional to a desired function, it suffices
to ensure the “detailed balance” condition. The main challenge is
showing rapid mixing of the Markov chain, i.e., the convergence rate
to the stationary distribution is bounded by a (small) polynomial
in the dimension and other relevant parameters.

The traditional analysis of Markov chains for sampling high-dimensional
distributions proceeds by analyzing the
conductance
of the
Markov chain, the minimum conditional escape probability over all
subsets of the state space of measure at most half. Bounding this
is done by relating probabilistic (one-step distribution) distance
to geometric distance, and then using a purely isoperimetric inequality
for subsets of the support. Indeed, this approach led to several interesting
questions and useful techniques, in particular the development of
isoperimetric inequalities and the discovery of (nearly) tight bounds
in many settings. To describe background and known results, let us
first define the sampling problem (readers familiar with the problem
can skip to §
1.1
).

Model and problems.

We assume access to an integrable logconcave function
\exp(-V(x))

in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, via an evaluation oracle for a convex function
V:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
.
We also assume that there exists a point
x_{0}
and parameters

r,R
such that for the distribution
\pi
with density
\mathrm{d}\pi\propto\exp(-V)\,\mathrm{d}x
,
a ball of radius
r
centered at
x_{0}
is contained in the level
set of
\pi
of measure
1/8
1
1

1

We use the standard convention of
1/8
; any constant bounded away
from
1
would suffice. In fact, we will use an even weaker condition;
see Problem
2.1
and Lemma
2.6
.
and
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}[\lVert X-x_{0}\rVert^{2}]\leq R^{2}
. We refer to this as
a
well-defined function oracle
2
2

2

Without loss of generality, we will assume by scaling that
r=1
.
,
denoted by
\mathsf{Eval}_{\mathcal{P}}(V)
where
\mathcal{P}
indicates access
to the actual values of parameters in
\mathcal{P}
(e.g.,
\mathsf{Eval}_{x_{0},R}(V)

presents both
x_{0}
and
R
, while
\mathsf{Eval}(V)
does not provide
any parameters).

Given this oracle, in this paper we consider three central problems:
(1) sample from the distribution
\pi
, (2) find an affine transformation
that places
\pi
in near-isotropic position, and (3) estimate the
integral
\int e^{-V(x)}\,\mathrm{d}x
(i.e., the normalization constant
of
\pi
). We measure the complexity in terms of the number of oracle
calls and the total number of arithmetic operations.

Complexity of logconcave sampling.

Sampling general logconcave functions, as an algorithmic problem,
was first studied by Applegate and Kannan
[
AK91
]
,
who established an algorithm with complexity polynomial in the dimension
assuming Lipschitzness of the function over its support. They did
this by creating a sufficiently fine grid (so that the function changed
by at most a small constant within each grid cell) and used a discrete
“grid walk”. They sampled a grid point and used rejection sampling
to output a point from the cube associated with the grid point. The
output distribution is guaranteed to be within desired total variation
(
\mathsf{TV}
) distance from the target distribution. Following many developments,
Lovász and Vempala improved the bound to scale as
n^{2}R^{2}/r^{2}

[
LV07
,
LV06a
]
. More specifically,
[
LV07
, Theorem 2.2, 2.3]

analyzed the
\mathsf{Ball\ Walk}
and
\mathsf{Hit\text{-}and\text{-}Run}
and proved a bound of

\frac{M^{4}}{\varepsilon^{4}}\frac{n^{2}R^{2}}{r^{2}}\log\frac{M}{\varepsilon}

steps/queries to reach a distribution within
\varepsilon

\mathsf{TV}
-distance
of the target starting from an
M
-warm distribution.
[
LV06a
, Theorem 1.1]

showed that
\mathsf{Hit\text{-}and\text{-}Run}
achieves the same guarantee in

\frac{n^{2}R^{2}}{r^{2}}\log^{\mathcal{O}(1)}\frac{nMR}{\varepsilon r}

steps. We note that in all previous work on general logconcave sampling
the output guarantees are in
\mathsf{TV}
-distance. It is often desirable
to have stronger guarantees such as the
\mathsf{KL}
or Rényi divergences.

Classical sampling algorithms.

For target density proportional to
e^{-V}
, the
\mathsf{Ball\ Walk}
with parameter

\delta
proceeds as follows: at a point
x
with
e^{-V(x)}>0
,
sample a uniform random point
y
in the ball of radius
\delta

centered at
x
; go to
y
with probability
\min(1,\frac{e^{-V(y)}}{e^{-V(x)}})
,
staying at
x
with the remaining probability.
\mathsf{Hit\text{-}and\text{-}Run}
does not need
a parameter: at current point
x
, pick a uniform random line
\ell

through
x
, and go to a random point
y
along
\ell
with probability
proportional to
e^{-V(y)}
(marginal along
\ell
). For technical
reasons, both of these walks (and almost all other walks) need to
be
lazy
, i.e., they do nothing with probability
1/2
, and
do the above step with probability
1/2
.

Sampling and isoperimetry.

The complexity of sampling is intuitively tied closely to the isoperimetry
of the target distribution. If the target has poor isoperimetry (roughly,
a small measure surface can partition the support into two large measure
subsets), then any “local” Markov chain will have difficulty moving
from one large subset to its complement. There are two alternative
views of isoperimetry — functional and geometric. We recap the
definitions of isoperimetric constants.

Definition 1.1
.

We say that a probability measure
\pi
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfies a

Poincaré inequality
(PI) with parameter
C_{\mathsf{PI}}(\pi)

if for all smooth functions
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
,

\operatorname{var}_{\pi}f\leq C_{\mathsf{PI}}(\pi)\,\mathbb{E}_{\pi}[\lVert\nabla f\rVert^{2}]\,,

(
\mathsf{PI}
)

where
\operatorname{var}_{\pi}f=\mathbb{E}_{\pi}[\lvert f-\mathbb{E}_{\pi}f\rvert^{2}]
.

The Poincaré inequality is implied by the generally stronger log-Sobolev
inequality.

Definition 1.2
.

We say that a probability measure
\pi
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfies a

log-Sobolev inequality
(LSI) with parameter
C_{\mathsf{LSI}}(\pi)

if for all smooth functions
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
,

\operatorname{Ent}_{\pi}(f^{2})\leq 2C_{\mathsf{LSI}}(\pi)\,\mathbb{E}_{\pi}[\lVert\nabla f\rVert^{2}]\,,

(
\mathsf{LSI}
)

where
\operatorname{Ent}_{\pi}(f^{2}):=\mathbb{E}_{\pi}[f^{2}\log f^{2}]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi}[f^{2}]\log\mathbb{E}_{\pi}[f^{2}]
.

For the geometric view, we define
\pi(\partial S):=\liminf_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\frac{\pi(S_{\varepsilon})-\pi(S)}{\varepsilon}
,
where
S_{\varepsilon}:=\{x:d(x,S)\leq\varepsilon\}
.

Definition 1.3
.

The
Cheeger constant

C_{\mathsf{Ch}}(\pi)
of a probability measure

\pi
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is defined as

C_{\mathsf{Ch}}(\pi)=\inf_{S\subset\mathbb{R}^{n},\pi(S)\leq\frac{1}{2}}\frac{\pi(\partial S)}{\pi(S)}\,.

Definition 1.4
.

The
log-Cheeger constant

C_{\mathsf{logCh}}(\pi)
of a probability measure

\pi
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is defined as

C_{\mathsf{logCh}}(\pi)=\inf_{S\subset\mathbb{R}^{n},\pi(S)\leq\frac{1}{2}}\frac{\pi(\partial S)}{\pi(S)\sqrt{\log(1/\pi(S))}}\,.

It is known that for logconcave measures,
C_{\mathsf{PI}}(\pi)=\Theta(C_{\mathsf{Ch}}^{-2}(\pi))

[
Mil09
]
and
C_{\mathsf{LSI}}(\pi)=\Theta(C_{\mathsf{logCh}}^{-2}(\pi))

[
Led94
]
.
Bounding these constants has been a major research topic for decades.
In recent years, following many improvements, it has been shown that
for isotropic logconcave measures,
C_{\mathsf{PI}}(\pi)\lesssim\log n

[
Kla23
]

and for isotropic logconcave ones with support of diameter
D
, we
have
C_{\mathsf{LSI}}(\pi)\lesssim D

[
LV24
]
. The former
is conjectured to be
\mathcal{O}(1)
(the KLS conjecture), and the latter
is the best possible.

The significance of these constants for algorithmic sampling became
clear with the analysis of the
\mathsf{Ball\ Walk}
, where the bound on its convergence
from a warm start to a logconcave distribution depends directly on

C_{\mathsf{Ch}}^{-2}(\pi)

[
KLS97
]
(this was the original
motivation for the KLS conjecture) — the mixing time of the
\mathsf{Ball\ Walk}

starting from an
M
-warm distribution to reach
\varepsilon

\mathsf{TV}
-distance
is bounded by
\mathcal{O}(n^{2}C_{\mathsf{Ch}}^{-2}\operatorname{poly}\frac{M}{\varepsilon})
. For the special
case of uniformly sampling convex bodies, using the notion of a
\mathsf{Speedy\ Walk}
,

this can be improved to
\mathcal{O}(Mn^{2}C_{\mathsf{Ch}}^{-2}\log\frac{M}{\varepsilon})
,
but using it in the analysis entails handling several technical difficulties.
In principle, this should be extendable to general logconcave functions,
albeit with formidable technical complications. We take a different
approach.

Sampling, isoperimetry, and diffusion.

The connection of isoperimetric constants to the convergence of continuous-time

diffusion
is a classical subject. The Langevin diffusion is
a canonical stochastic differential equation (SDE) given by

\mathrm{d}X_{t}=-\nabla V(X_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}\,\mathrm{d}B_{t}\quad X_{0}\sim\nu_{0}\,,

where
B_{t}
is the standard Brownian process, and
V
is a smooth
function. Then, under mild conditions, the law
\nu_{t}
of
X_{t}

converges to the distribution with density
\nu
proportional to

e^{-V}
in the limit. Moreover, it can be shown that

\chi^{2}(\nu_{t}\mathbin{\|}\nu)\leq\exp\bigl(-\frac{2t}{C_{\mathsf{PI}}(\nu)}\bigr)\,\chi^{2}(\nu_{0}\mathbin{\|}\nu)\,,\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{KL}(\nu_{t}\mathbin{\|}\nu)\leq\exp\bigl(-\frac{2t}{C_{\mathsf{LSI}}(\nu)}\bigr)\,\mathsf{KL}(\nu_{0}\mathbin{\|}\nu)\,.

A natural idea for a sampling algorithm is to discretize the diffusion
equation. This discretized algorithm was shown to converge in
\text{poly}(n,\beta,C_{\mathsf{PI}}(\nu))

and
\text{poly}(n,\beta,C_{\mathsf{LSI}}(\nu))
iterations under (

PI

)

[
CEL+22
]
and (

LSI

)
[
VW19
]

respectively, assuming
\beta
-Lipschitzness of
\nabla V
. Can
this approach be extended to sampling general logconcave densities
without the gradient Lipschitzness?

Algorithmic diffusion.

By
algorithmic diffusion
, we mean the general idea of discretizing
a diffusion process and proving guarantees on the discretization error
and query complexity. Recently,
[
KVZ24
]
showed that
such an algorithm (called
\mathsf{\mathsf{In\text{-}and\text{-}Out}}
there), which can be viewed as a

\mathsf{Proximal\ Sampler}
, works for sampling from the uniform distribution over a convex
body and recovers state-of-the-art complexity guarantees with substantially
improved output guarantees (Rényi-divergences). The guarantee was
extended to the Rényi-infinity (or pointwise) distance as well

[
KZ25
]
. A nice aspect of the analysis is that it shows
convergence directly in terms of isoperimetric constants of target
distributions. As a result, it provides a unifying point of view for
analysis without requiring the use of technically sophisticated tools
such as the
\mathsf{Speedy\ Walk}
and
s
-conductance
[
LS93
,
KLS97
,
LV07
]
.
We discuss the approach in more detail presently.

This brings us to the main motivation of the current paper:
Can
we use algorithmic diffusion to obtain faster algorithms with stronger
guarantees for sampling, rounding, and integrating general logconcave
functions?

1.1
Results

In this paper, we propose new approaches for general logconcave sampling,
rounding and integration, leading to the first improvements in the
complexity of these problems in nearly two decades
[
LV06a
]
.
Our methods crucially rely on a reduction from general logconcave
sampling to
exponential sampling
in one higher dimension. With
this reduction in hand, we develop a new framework for sampling and
improve the complexity of four fundamental problems: (1) logconcave
sampling from an
\mathcal{O}(1)
-warm start, (2) warm-start generation, (3)
isotropic rounding and (4) integration. For each problem, our improved
complexity for general logconcave distributions matches the current
best complexity for the uniform distribution over a convex body, a
setting that has received much attention for more than three decades

[
DFK91
,
LS90
,
Lov91
,
LS93
,
KLS97
,
LV06b
,
CV18
,
KVZ24
,
KZ25
]
.
We now present our main results, followed by a detailed discussion
of the challenges and techniques in §
1.2
, and
notations and preliminaries in §
1.3
.

Result 1: Logconcave sampling from a warm start.

When a logconcave distribution
\pi^{X}\propto e^{-V}
is presented
with the evaluation oracle for
V
, we instead consider the “lifted”
distribution

\pi(x,t)\propto\exp(-nt)\cdot\mathds{1}[(x,t):V(x)\leq nt]

in the augmented
(x,t)
-space, the
X
-marginal of which is exactly

\pi^{X}
(see Proposition
2.3
). We quantify
in §
2
how this affects parameters pertinent
to sampling (e.g., isoperimetric constants).

Input:
initial pt.
z_{0}\sim\pi_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
,

\mathcal{K}=\{(x,t):V(x)\leq nt\}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, threshold
N
, variance

h
.

Output:

z_{k+1}=(x_{k+1},t_{k+1})
.

1:

for

i=0,\dotsc,k

do

2:

Sample
y_{i+1}\sim\mathcal{N}(z_{i},hI_{n+1})
.

3:

Sample
z_{i+1}\sim\mathcal{N}(y_{i+1}-hne_{n+1},hI_{n+1})|_{\mathcal{K}}
.

4:

\quad(\uparrow)

Repeat
z_{i+1}\sim\mathcal{N}(y_{i+1}-hne_{n+1},hI_{n+1})

until
z_{i+1}\in\mathcal{K}
. If
\#
attempts
{}_{i}\,\geq N
, declare

Failure
.

5:

end

for

Algorithm 1

\mathsf{Proximal\ Sampler}

\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{exp}}

To sample from the exponential target
\pi
using a given
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}

warm-start, we use the
\mathsf{Proximal\ Sampler}
(see Figure
1.1
) with an
evaluation oracle that returns
nt
conditioned on
V(x)\leq nt
.
One iteration of the sampler is reversible with respect to the stationary
distribution
\pi
, while it can be interpreted as a composition
of two complimentary diffusion processes. Discarding the
T
-component
of a sample, we are left with the
X
-component with law close to
our desired target
\pi^{X}
. In this regard, our sampler is a natural
generalization of
\mathsf{\mathsf{In\text{-}and\text{-}Out}}

[
KVZ24
]
, the
\mathsf{Proximal\ Sampler}
for uniform
sampling. Concretely, we establish mixing guarantees and query complexities
of our sampler for the target logconcave distribution
\pi^{X}
in
terms of its Poincaré constant (which in general satisfies
\lVert\operatorname{Cov}\pi^{X}\rVert\leq C_{\mathsf{PI}}(\pi^{X})\lesssim\lVert\operatorname{Cov}\pi^{X}\rVert\log n

[
Kla23
]
).

Theorem 1.5
.

For any logconcave distribution
\pi^{X}

specified by a well-defined function oracle
\mathsf{Eval}(V)
, for any given

\eta,\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
q\geq 2
, and
\pi_{0}^{X}
with
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}(\pi_{0}^{X}\mathbin{\|}\pi^{X})=\log M
,
we can use the
\mathsf{Proximal\ Sampler}

\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{exp}}
(Algorithm
1
) with
suitable choices of parameters, so that with probability at least

1-\eta
, we obtain a sample
X
such that
\mathcal{R}_{q}(\operatorname{law}X\mathbin{\|}\pi^{X})\leq\varepsilon
,
using
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(qMn^{2}\,(\lVert\operatorname{Cov}\pi^{X}\rVert\vee 1)\operatorname{polylog}\frac{1}{\eta\varepsilon})

evaluation queries in expectation.

See §
2.2.1
for a more detailed description of
the heat flow perspective and its benefits for sampling. Our specific
setting of parameters can be found in Theorem
2.15
.
In comparison with the previous best complexity of
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{2}\operatorname{tr}(\operatorname{Cov}\pi^{X})\operatorname{polylog}\frac{M}{\varepsilon})
,
which is for
\mathsf{TV}
-distance, the
\mathsf{Proximal\ Sampler}

\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{exp}}
achieves a provably
better rate (since
\lVert\operatorname{Cov}\pi^{X}\rVert\leq\operatorname{tr}(\operatorname{Cov}\pi^{X})
) from
an
\mathcal{O}(1)
-warm start, and moreover does so in general
\mathcal{R}_{q}
-divergences.
This mixing rate matches the previously known best rate for the uniform
sampling by the
\mathsf{Ball\ Walk}

[
KLS97
]
as well as
\mathsf{\mathsf{In\text{-}and\text{-}Out}}

[
KVZ24
]
.

Figure 1.1
:
Reduction to an exponential distribution and sampling via the
\mathsf{Proximal\ Sampler}

\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{exp}}
.

Result 2: Warm-start generation (sampling without a warm start).

The
\mathsf{Proximal\ Sampler}

\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{exp}}
assumes access to a warm start in
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}
,
and generating such a good warm-start is an important and challenging
algorithmic problem in its own right. In §
3
,
we propose
\mathsf{Tilted\ Gaussian\ Cooling}
which generates an
\mathcal{O}(1)
-warm start in the
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}
-divergence
for any target logconcave distribution. This algorithm generalizes

\mathsf{Gaussian\ Cooling}

[
CV18
]
, the known method for generating
a warm-start for the uniform distribution over a convex body. The
high-level idea is to follow a sequence
\{\mu_{i}\}_{i\in[m]}
of
distributions, where
\mu_{1}
is easy to sample from,
\mu_{i}

and
\mu_{i+1}
are close in some probability divergences, and
\mu_{m}

is the desired target.

Our algorithm first reduces the original target to the exponential
distribution and then follows annealing distributions of the form

\mu_{\sigma^{2},\rho}(x,t)\propto\exp\bigl(-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2}-\rho t\bigr)\cdot\mathds{1}[\{V(x)\leq nt\}\cap\{\lVert x\rVert=\mathcal{O}(R),\lvert t\rvert=\mathcal{O}(1)\}]\,,

with a carefully chosen schedule for updating the parameters
\sigma^{2}

and
\rho
. Roughly speaking, we update the
X
-marginal through

\mathsf{Gaussian\ Cooling}
and the
T
-marginal using an exponential
tilt
. In
order to move across the annealing distributions,
\mathsf{Tilted\ Gaussian\ Cooling}
requires
an efficient sampler for the intermediate annealing distributions,
ideally with guarantees in the
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}
-divergence in order
to relay
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}
-warmness guarantees along the annealing
scheme. We use the
\mathsf{Proximal\ Sampler}
for these intermediate distributions with
a
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}
-divergence guarantee, namely the
\mathsf{Proximal\ Sampler}

\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{ann}}

(Algorithm
3
) in §
3.2
.
In our computational model, this sampler started at a previous annealing
distribution returns a sample with law
\mu
satisfying
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}(\mu\mathbin{\|}\mu_{\sigma^{2},\rho})\leq\varepsilon
,
using
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{2}\sigma^{2}\operatorname{polylog}\nicefrac{{R}}{{\eta\varepsilon}})
evaluation
queries in expectation (see Theorem
3.11
).

By sampling these annealing distributions through
\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{ann}}
,
\mathsf{Tilted\ Gaussian\ Cooling}

obtains an
\mathcal{O}(1)
-warm start for
\pi
(and hence for the desired
target
\pi^{X}
); then runs the
\mathsf{Proximal\ Sampler}

\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{exp}}
one final time
to obtain a sample with
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}
-guarantees (this is a very
strong notion of probability divergence that recovers all commonly
used distances such as
\mathsf{TV},\mathsf{KL},\chi^{2},
or
\mathcal{R}_{q}
).

Theorem 1.6
.

For any logconcave distributions
\pi^{X}

specified by
\mathsf{Eval}_{x_{0},R}(V)
, for any given
\eta,\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,

\mathsf{Tilted\ Gaussian\ Cooling}
(Algorithm
2
) with probability at least
1-\eta
,
returns a sample with law
\nu
such that
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}(\nu\mathbin{\|}\pi^{X})\leq\varepsilon
,
using
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{2}(R^{2}\vee n)\operatorname{polylog}\nicefrac{{1}}{{\eta\varepsilon}})

evaluation queries in expectation. Hence, if
\pi^{X}
is well-rounded
(i.e.,
R^{2}\lesssim n
), then
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{3}\operatorname{polylog}\nicefrac{{1}}{{\eta\varepsilon}})

queries suffice.

This improves the prior best complexity of
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{3}R^{2}\operatorname{polylog}\frac{1}{\varepsilon})

for general logconcave sampling (in
\mathsf{TV}
-distance) from scratch
by
[
LV06a
, Collorary 1.2]
, and provides a much stronger

\mathcal{R}_{\infty}
-guarantee. Moreover, this complexity matches the
best-known complexity for uniform sampling with the
\mathsf{TV}
-guarantee
by
[
CV18
]
and with the
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}
-guarantee
by
[
KZ25
]
.

Result 3: Isotropic rounding of logconcave distributions.

The above results on the complexity of logconcave sampling still have
dependence on the second moment
R^{2}
, so those are not fully polynomial
in the problem parameters. In §
4
, we address this
issue by initially running an algorithm for isotropic rounding which
makes the covariance matrix of a given logconcave distribution near-isotropic
(i.e.,
\operatorname{Cov}\pi^{X}\approx I_{n}
). After this rounding,
R^{2}=\mathcal{O}(n)
,
and thus the guarantee above turns into
n^{3}
at the cost of a
multiplicative factor of
\operatorname{polylog}R
in the complexity. Isotropic
rounding is also a useful tool for many other high-dimensional algorithms.

At a high level, just as in the warm-start generation, our algorithm
follows a sequence
\{\mu_{i}\}
of distributions while updating
an affine map
F:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
along the way. An important subroutine
is to convert a well-rounded distribution (i.e., with
\operatorname{tr}(\operatorname{Cov})=\mathcal{O}(n)
)
to one that is near-isotropic (i.e., with
\operatorname{Cov}\approx I_{n}
). To
this end, we generalize the approach taken in
[
JLLV21
]

for the uniform distribution — repeatedly [draw a few samples

\to
compute a crude estimate of the covariance
\to
identify
skewed directions from the estimation and upscale them]. In doing
so, we rely crucially on our sampler’s mixing rate in terms of
\lVert\operatorname{Cov}\pi^{X}\rVert

(instead of
\operatorname{tr}(\operatorname{Cov}\pi^{X})
) in
Result 1
and its improved
complexity for warm-start generation in
Result 2
.

Theorem 1.7
.

For any logconcave distribution
\pi^{X}

specified by
\mathsf{Eval}_{x_{0},R}(V)
, there exists a randomized algorithm
with query complexity of
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{3.5}\operatorname{polylog}R)
that finds
an affine map
F
such that the pushforward of
\pi^{X}
via
F

is
1.01
-isotropic with probability at least
1-\mathcal{O}(n^{-1/2})
.

Our complexity improves upon the previous best complexity of
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{4}\operatorname{polylog}R)

for general logconcave distributions
[
LV06a
, Theorem 6.1]
.
Moreover, our rate matches that of isotropic rounding for the uniform
distribution
[
JLLV21
,
JLLV24
]
.

Result 4: Integration of logconcave functions.

Finally, we examine the complexity of integrating a logconcave function,
a classical application of logconcave sampling. To this end, we extend
an annealing approach in
[
CV18
]
to general logconcave
integration in §
5
. Similar to
\mathsf{Tilted\ Gaussian\ Cooling}
, we follow
a sequence
\{f_{i}\}_{i\in[m]}
of logconcave functions, where
f_{1}

is easy to integrate,
\mu_{i}\propto f_{i}
is an
\mathcal{O}(1)
-warm
start for
\mu_{i+1}\propto f_{i+1}
, the variance of
f_{i+1}/f_{i}

with respect to
\mu_{i}
is small enough (e.g.,
\chi^{2}(\mu_{i+1}\mathbin{\|}\mu_{i})=\mathcal{O}(m^{-1})
),
and
f_{m}
is the target logconcave function.

Theorem 1.8
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
and an integrable
well-rounded logconcave function
f=e^{-V}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
presented
by
\mathsf{Eval}_{x_{0},R}(V)
, there exists an algorithm that with probability
at least
3/4
, returns a
(1+\varepsilon)
-multiplicative approximation
to the integral of
f
using
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{3}/\varepsilon^{2})
queries.
For an arbitrary logconcave
f
given by a well-defined function
oracle, the complexity is bounded by
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{3.5}\operatorname{polylog}R+n^{3}/\varepsilon^{2})
.

This result improves the previous best complexity
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{4}/\varepsilon^{2})

of integrating logconcave functions
[
LV06a
, Theorem 1.3]
.
Again, our improved complexity matches that for the uniform distribution
(i.e., volume computation)
[
CV18
,
JLLV21
,
JLLV24
]
.
We note that due to the strong guarantees of the new sampler, we can
streamline the analysis of errors and dependence among samples used
to estimate
f_{i+1}/f_{i}
, simplifying and strengthening earlier
analyses
[
KLS97
,
LV06c
,
CV18
]
.

1.2
Techniques and background

In proving our results — faster algorithms for the basic problems
of sampling (with and without a warm start), rounding and integration
— we do not take the most direct route. Instead we develop several
techniques that appear to be interesting and we imagine will be useful
in other contexts as well. These include the reversible heat-flow
perspective for the design of polynomial-time algorithms providing
a direct connection to isoperimetric constants, sampling guarantees
in the strong notion of
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}
-distance (which has other
motivations such as differential privacy), relevant geometry of logconcave
functions, and a streamlined analysis of estimation errors of dependent
samples.

1.2.1
Going beyond the uniform distribution

The uniform distribution over a convex body is a special case of
a logconcave distribution. The best-known polynomial complexity for
uniform sampling is achieved by addressing three subproblems: (1)
sampling from a warm start, (2) generating a warm start and (3) performing
isotropic rounding. Extending the best-known guarantees and algorithms
for each of these problems to general logconcave densities is the
primary challenged addressed in this paper. In our proofs, we use
general properties of logconcave functions and avoid any structural
assumptions on the target density.

The first challenge is to establish a logconcave sampler with a mixing
rate matching that of the best uniform samplers, such as the
\mathsf{Ball\ Walk}

[
KLS97
]
or
\mathsf{\mathsf{In\text{-}and\text{-}Out}}

[
KVZ24
]
. These uniform
samplers have a mixing rate of
n^{2}\lVert\operatorname{Cov}\pi^{X}\rVert
, while
\mathsf{Hit\text{-}and\text{-}Run}

in
[
LV06a
]
, previously the best for general logconcave
distributions, has a rate of
n^{2}\operatorname{tr}(\operatorname{Cov}\pi^{X})
. The improved
complexity bounds of these uniform samplers come from a better understanding
of isoperimetric properties, such as the Cheeger and Poincaré constants
for logconcave distributions, and this aspect is more direct for
\mathsf{\mathsf{In\text{-}and\text{-}Out}}
.
Here we extend
\mathsf{\mathsf{In\text{-}and\text{-}Out}}
to general logconcave distributions. As we
elaborate in §
1.2.2
, this requires implementing
rejection sampling for the distributions of the form
\exp(-V(x)-\frac{1}{2h}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2})

(for a suitable
h
) using
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(1)
queries. However, with only
logconcavity assumed for
e^{-V}
, determining a suitable proposal
distribution and analyzing its query complexity present significant
challenges.

Reduction to an exponential distribution.

To extend methods from uniform to general logconcave distributions,
we leverage a conceptual connection between logconcave sampling and
convex optimization — sampling from
\exp(-V)
is analogous to
minimization of
V
. In optimization, we bound
V(x)
with a new
variable
t
, and add the convex constraint
V(x)\leq t
. Inspired
by this, we consider an exponential distribution with density
\pi(x,t)\propto\exp(-nt)\cdot\mathds{1}[(x,t):V(x)\leq nt]

on
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, with
X
-marginal
\pi^{X}\propto\exp(-V)
(Proposition
2.3
).
The scaling of
n
in the exponential function is a natural choice
as we will see later.

This reduction offers several advantages. First, the potential
V

becomes linear, opening up a possible extension of previous analyses.
Second, it points to a clear path to generalizing ideas/guarantees
devised for uniform sampling. Specifically, the conditional law
\pi^{X|T=t}

of
X
given
T=t
is the uniform distribution over the convex level
set
L_{nt}=\{V(x)\leq nt\}
. Thus,
\pi
can be interpreted as
an average of the uniform distribution over
L_{nt}
weighted by

e^{-nt}
, and this idea is crucial to our subsequent algorithms.

A similar exponential reduction was discussed conceptually in
[
KLSV22
]

and developed further in
[
KV24
]
for a sampling analogue
of the interior-point method from optimization. Here, however, we
apply this reduction in our general setup, without specific assumptions
on the epigraph, and analyze structural properties of the reduced
distribution. For example, we bound the mean and variance along the

T
-direction. This allows us to analyze how this reduction impacts
key parameters of the original logconcave distribution, including
the largest eigenvalue and trace of the covariance.

1.2.2
Logconcave sampling from a warm start

Sampling through diffusion.

The proximal sampler
[
LST21
]
is essentially a Gibbs
sampler from two conditional distributions. For a target distribution

\pi^{X}\propto\exp(-V)
, it introduces a new variable
Y
and considers
the augmented distribution
\pi^{X,Y}\propto\exp(-V(x)-\frac{1}{2h}\,\lVert x-y\rVert^{2})

with parameter
h>0
. One iteration involves two steps: (i)
y\sim\pi^{Y|X=x}=\mathcal{N}(x,hI_{n})

and (ii)
x\sim\pi^{X|Y=y}\propto\exp(-V(\cdot)-\frac{1}{2h}\,\lVert\cdot-y\rVert^{2})
.
While step (i) is straightforward, step (ii) requires a nontrivial
sampling procedure.

The complexity of the
\mathsf{Proximal\ Sampler}
involves a mixing analysis (to determine
the number of iterations for a desired accuracy to the target) and
the query complexity of implementing step (ii). For the first part,

[
CCSW22
]
demonstrated that one iteration corresponds
to simulating the heat flow for (i) and then a
time reversal

of the heat flow for (ii) (see §
2.2.1
for details).
This leads to exponential decay of the
\mathcal{R}_{q}
-divergence, with
the decay rate dependent on the isoperimetry of the target distribution
(e.g, (

PI

) and (

LSI

)). For the second part, prior
approaches typically use rejection sampling or a different logconcave
sampler (e.g.,
\mathsf{MALA}
,
\mathsf{ULMC}
) under additional assumptions
such as the smoothness of
V
or access to first-order and proximal
oracles for
V
.

Sampling without smoothness.

Previous studies of the
\mathsf{Proximal\ Sampler}
focused on smooth unconstrained distributions,
where the potential satisfies
\nabla^{2}V\preceq\beta I_{n}
for some

\beta<\infty
, leaving open the complexities of uniform or general
logconcave sampling with hard constraints.
[
KVZ24
]
introduced

\mathsf{\mathsf{In\text{-}and\text{-}Out}}
, a version of the
\mathsf{Proximal\ Sampler}
for uniform sampling over convex
bodies. With
V(x)=(\mathds{1}[x\in\mathcal{K}])^{-1}
for a convex body
\mathcal{K}
,
one iteration of
\mathsf{\mathsf{In\text{-}and\text{-}Out}}
draws
y\sim\mathcal{N}(x,hI_{n})
and then
x\sim\mathcal{N}(y,hI_{n})|_{\mathcal{K}}

(the Gaussian truncated to
\mathcal{K}
) using rejection sampling on the
proposal
\mathcal{N}(y,hI_{n})
. They introduce a
threshold
parameter
on the number of rejection trials which ensures that the algorithm
does not use too many queries.

This diffusion-based approach turns out to be stronger than the
\mathsf{Ball\ Walk}
,
the previous best uniform sampler with query complexity
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{2}\lVert\operatorname{Cov}\pi^{X}\rVert\operatorname{polylog}\frac{1}{\varepsilon})

for obtaining an
\varepsilon
-close sample in
\mathsf{TV}
-distance.
\mathsf{\mathsf{In\text{-}and\text{-}Out}}

provides
\mathcal{R}_{q}
-divergence guarantees with a matching rate of

n^{2}\lVert\operatorname{Cov}\pi^{X}\rVert
for
\mathsf{TV}
-distance, and its analysis is
simpler than the
\mathsf{Ball\ Walk}
. Since the latter’s analysis in
[
KLS97
]

goes through its biased version (called the
\mathsf{Speedy\ Walk}
), it involves an
understanding of an additional rejection step for making the biased
distribution close to the uniform target, as well as the isoperimetric
constant of the biased distribution. In contrast,
\mathsf{\mathsf{In\text{-}and\text{-}Out}}
achieves
direct contraction towards the uniform target, with rate dependent
on isoperimetric constants of the original target, not a biased one,
and achieves stronger output guarantees.
\mathsf{\mathsf{In\text{-}and\text{-}Out}}
’s approach has also
been extended to truncated Gaussian sampling
[
KZ25
]
,
leading to a
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}
-guarantee that improves upon the
\mathsf{TV}
-guarantee
of the
\mathsf{Ball\ Walk}

[
CV18
]
.

Proximal sampler for general logconcave distributions.

This prompts a natural question if we can extend
\mathsf{\mathsf{In\text{-}and\text{-}Out}}
beyond constant
and quadratic potentials. Prior studies of the
\mathsf{Proximal\ Sampler}
by
[
CCSW22
,
KVZ24
]

allow for an immediate mixing analysis through the isoperimetry of
logconcave distributions. However, we also need a query complexity
bound for sampling from
\pi^{X|Y=y}\propto\exp(-V(x)-\frac{1}{2h}\,\lVert x-y\rVert^{2})
.
While one might consider using
\mathsf{Hit\text{-}and\text{-}Run}

[
LV06a
]
for
the second step, it requires
roundedness
of this distribution,
and its current complexity is also unsatisfactory. Rejection sampling
is another option, but without smoothness assumptions for general
logconcave distributions, it is challenging to identify an appropriate
proposal distribution and to bound the expected number of trials.

To handle an arbitrary convex potential
V
, we apply the exponential
reduction, turning
V
into a convex constraint and work with the
linear potential
nt
in one higher dimension. Extending
\mathsf{\mathsf{In\text{-}and\text{-}Out}}

to this exponential distribution, we propose the
\mathsf{Proximal\ Sampler}

\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{exp}}

(Algorithm
1
). Since
C_{\mathsf{PI}}(\pi)\lesssim(C_{\mathsf{PI}}(\pi^{X})+1)\log n

(Lemma
2.5
), the mixing rate of
\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{exp}}
for
\pi

is close to that of the
\mathsf{Proximal\ Sampler}
for
\pi^{X}
. Implementing the second
step is now simpler due to the linear potential, allowing us to extend
the analysis of
\mathsf{\mathsf{In\text{-}and\text{-}Out}}
for uniform and truncated Gaussian distributions
to exponential distributions (Lemma
2.14
). A new
technical ingredient is bounding the rate of increase of
\int_{\mathcal{K}_{\delta}}e^{-nt}/\int_{\mathcal{K}}e^{-nt}

as
\delta
grows, where
\mathcal{K}_{\delta}=\{z\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}:d(z,\mathcal{K})\leq\delta\}
.
We show that this growth rate is bounded by
e^{\delta n}
(Lemma
2.6

and
2.13
).

1.2.3
Warm-start generation

Prior work on warm-start generation
[
KLS97
,
LV06c
,
CV18
]

is based on using a sequence
\{\mu_{i}\}_{i\in[m]}
of distributions,
where
\mu_{1}
is easy to sample (e.g., uniform distribution over
a unit ball), each
\mu_{i}
is close to
\mu_{i+1}
in probabilistic
distance, and
\mu_{m}
is the target. By moving along this sequence
with a suitable sampler for intermediate annealing distributions,
one can generate a warm start for the desired distribution. This approach
is more efficient than trying to directly go from
\mu_{1}
to
\mu_{m}
.

Warm-start generation for uniform distributions.

The state-of-the-art algorithm for uniform distributions over convex
bodies is
\mathsf{Gaussian\ Cooling}

[
CV18
]
. They set
\mu_{i}(x)\propto\exp(-\lVert x\rVert^{2}/(2\sigma_{i}^{2}))
,
where
\sigma_{i}^{2}
increases from
n^{-1}
to
R^{2}
according
to a suitable schedule. This approach ensures that
\mu_{i}
is
\mathcal{O}(1)
-warm
with respect to
\mu_{i+1}
, and uses the
\mathsf{Ball\ Walk}
to sample from truncated
Gaussians with
\mathsf{TV}
-guarantees. Using a coupling argument, they
showed that with high probability, this scheme outputs an
\varepsilon
-close
sample to the uniform distribution in
\mathsf{TV}
-distance, using
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{2}(R^{2}\vee n)\operatorname{polylog}\frac{1}{\varepsilon})

membership queries.
[
KZ25
]
later improved this by replacing
the
\mathsf{Ball\ Walk}
with the
\mathsf{Proximal\ Sampler}
, achieving
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}
-guarantees
with the same complexity. As a result,
\mathsf{Gaussian\ Cooling}
transfers
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}
-warmness
across the sequence of distributions, and thus the complexity for
uniform sampling from a convex body remains the same even for
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}
-divergence
guarantees.

Going beyond uniform distributions.

In
[
CV18
]
, Cousins and Vempala raised the question
of whether their annealing strategy can be extended to arbitrary logconcave
distributions with complexity
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{2}(R^{2}\vee n))
. A natural
choice for annealing distributions is
\mu(x)\propto\exp(-V(x)-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2})
,
which would still provide
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}
-closeness of consecutive
distributions and allow for accelerated updates to
\sigma^{2}
.
However, prior samplers lack the necessary guarantees for these distributions,
so we use the exponential reduction.

To generate a warm start for
\pi(x,t)\propto e^{-nt}|_{\mathcal{K}}
, it
seems natural to consider an annealing distribution obtained by multiplying

\pi
by a Gaussian in “
(x,t)
” for a direct application of
\mathsf{Gaussian\ Cooling}

in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. However, due to different rates of changes in the
quadratic term and linear term in
t
over an interval of length

\mathcal{O}(1)
, these two terms do not properly cancel each other, which
implies that the warmness of
\mu_{m-1}
with respect to
\mu_{m}=\pi

is no longer
\mathcal{O}(1)
-bounded.

We address this by introducing
\mu_{\sigma^{2},\rho}(x,t)\propto\exp(-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2}-\rho t)\cdot\mathds{1}[(x,t)\in\bar{\mathcal{K}}]

where
\sigma^{2}\in(0,R^{2}]
,
\rho\in(0,n]
, and
\bar{\mathcal{K}}=\{V(x)\leq nt\}\cap\{\lVert x\rVert=\mathcal{O}(R),\lVert t\rVert=\mathcal{O}(1)\}
.
Essentially, this runs
\mathsf{Gaussian\ Cooling}
along the
x
-direction with an exponential
tilt in the
t
-direction, ensuring that consecutive distributions
are
\mathcal{O}(1)
-close in
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}
. However, we need an efficient
sampler for
\mu_{\sigma^{2},\rho}
with
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}
-guarantees
to maintain
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}
-warmness across the annealing scheme.

Sampling from annealing distributions.

Given the form of the annealing distribution (the potential is a combination
of linear and quadratic terms), we use the
\mathsf{Proximal\ Sampler}
to develop
\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{ann}}

(Algorithm
3
). The query complexity for rejection
sampling in the second step now can be derived from our analysis of

\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{exp}}
(Lemma
2.14
) and the
\mathsf{Proximal\ Sampler}
for truncated
Gaussians
[
KZ25
]
. For mixing with
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}

guarantees, we apply a technique from
[
KZ25
]
. We obtain
a mixing rate of
\mathcal{O}(h^{-1}C_{\mathsf{LSI}}(\mu_{\sigma^{2},\rho}))
for the

\mathsf{Proximal\ Sampler}
based on (

LSI

) (Proposition
2.7
).
This results in only a
doubly logarithmic
dependence on the
initial warmness, implying convergence from any
feasible
start
with an overhead of
\operatorname{polylog}(n,R)
(Lemma
3.5
).
This
uniform ergodicity
implies
L^{\infty}
-norm contraction
of density toward the target
[
DMLM03
]
(see Lemma
3.4
),
leading to a
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}
-guarantee of
\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{ann}}
without significant
overhead (Theorem
3.16
).

In Lemma
3.3
, we bound the LSI constant of the
annealing distribution
\mu_{\sigma^{2},\rho}
by
\sigma^{2}\vee 1

via the Bakry-Émery criterion (Lemma
A.1
) and
Holley–Stroock perturbation principle (Lemma
A.2
).
For
\nu\propto\exp(-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2}-\frac{t^{2}}{2}-\rho t)|_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}
,
since the potential of
\nu
is
\min(\sigma^{-2},1)
-strongly convex,
its LSI without convex truncation is bounded by
\sigma^{2}\vee 1

through Bakry-Émery, and convex truncation to
\bar{\mathcal{K}}
only
helps in satisfying the criterion
[
BGL14
]
. Also,
as
\sup t-\inf t=\Theta(1)
over
\bar{\mathcal{K}}
, the ratio of
\mu_{\sigma^{2},\rho}

to
\nu
is bounded below and above by
\Theta(1)
, so the perturbation
principle ensures that
C_{\mathsf{LSI}}(\mu_{\sigma^{2},\rho})\lesssim C_{\mathsf{LSI}}(\nu)\leq\sigma^{2}\vee 1
.

Gaussian cooling with exponential tilt.

With the query complexity of
\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{ann}}
in mind, we design
\mathsf{Tilted\ Gaussian\ Cooling}

(Algorithm
2
) for warm-start generation for
\pi(x,t)\propto\exp(-nt)|_{\mathcal{K}}
.
We run rejection sampling with proposal
\mathcal{N}(0,n^{-1}I_{n})\otimes\text{Unif}\,(I_{t})

for some interval
I_{t}
of length
\Theta(1)
, and initial distribution

\exp(-\frac{n}{2}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2})|_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}
, which is
\mathcal{O}(1)
-warm
with respect to
\mu_{1}=\exp(-\frac{n}{2}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2}-t)|_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}
.
In Phase I, we update the two parameters according to
\sigma^{2}\leftarrow\sigma^{2}(1+n^{-1})

and
\rho\leftarrow\rho(1+n^{-1})
while
\rho\leq n
and
\sigma^{2}\leq 1
.
Since Phase I involves
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n)
inner phases with complexities
of sampling from each annealing distributions being
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{2}(\sigma^{2}\vee 1))=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{2})
,
the total complexity is
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{3})
. In Phase II, we accelerate

\sigma^{2}
-updates via
\sigma^{2}\leftarrow\sigma^{2}(1+\sigma^{2}/R^{2})

as in
\mathsf{Gaussian\ Cooling}
. With
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(R^{2}/\sigma^{2})
inner phases (for
doubling of
\sigma^{2}
) and sampling complexity
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{2}\sigma^{2})

per inner phase, this has total complexity
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{2}R^{2})
.
At termination,
\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{exp}}
is run with
\mu_{R^{2},n}
as the initial
distribution for target
\bar{\pi}\propto e^{-nt}|_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}
, where
these two are close in
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}
. Using the LSI of
\bar{\pi}

and the boosting scheme again, we can achieve an
\varepsilon
-close sample
to
\pi
(not
\bar{\pi}
) in the
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}
-divergence,
using
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{2}(R^{2}\vee n)\operatorname{polylog}\frac{1}{\varepsilon})
queries
in total (Lemma
3.16
).

1.2.4
Rounding

Rounding is the key to reducing the dependence on
R
from
\operatorname{poly}R

to
\operatorname{polylog}R
.

Isotropic rounding for uniform distributions.

The previous best rounding algorithm for uniform distributions, proposed
by
[
JLLV21
]
, gradually isotropizes a sequence
\{\mu_{i}\}

of distributions. For a convex body
\mathcal{K}
, they set
\mu_{i}=\text{Unif}\,(\mathcal{K}\cap B_{\delta^{i}}(0))

for
\delta=1+n^{-1/2}
, increasing
i
while
\delta^{i}\leq R
.
Their approach entails two important tasks:
Outer loop
:
if
F(\mathcal{K}\cap B_{r})
is near-isotropic for an affine map
F
, then
show that
F(\mathcal{K}\cap B_{\delta r})
is well-rounded, and
Inner loop
:
design an algorithm of
n^{3}
-complexity that isotropizes a given
well-rounded uniform distribution. The first task was accomplished
by
[
JLLV24
]
through Paouris’ lemma
(i.e., exponential tail decay) and a universal property that the diameter
of an isotropic convex body is bounded by
n+1
.

The second task was addressed by repeating [draw samples
\to

compute crude covariance estimation
\to
upscale skewed directions
of the covariance estimation]. They first run
\mathsf{Gaussian\ Cooling}
to obtain a
warm start for a uniform distribution
\mu
from a convex body. Then,
when the inner radius is
r
, the
\mathsf{Ball\ Walk}
(or
\mathsf{\mathsf{In\text{-}and\text{-}Out}}
) is used to
generate
r^{2}
samples approximately distributed according to
\mu
.
These
r^{2}
samples give a rough estimate of the covariance matrix

\overline{\Sigma}
of
\mu
such that
\lvert\overline{\Sigma}-\Sigma\rvert\precsim nI_{n}
,
where
\Sigma=\operatorname{Cov}\mu
. Since the query complexity of these uniform
samplers is
n^{2}\lVert\Sigma\rVert/r^{2}
, this procedure uses
n^{2}\lVert\Sigma\rVert

queries in total. Then, it computes the eigenvalue/vectors of
\overline{\Sigma}

and scales up (by a factor of
2)
the subspace spanned by eigenvectors
with eigenvalues less than
n
. One iteration of this process achieves
two key properties: the largest eigenvalue of the covariance
\Sigma

increases by at most
n
additively while
r=\operatorname{inrad}\mu

almost

doubles. Since the well-roundedness ensures that
\lVert\Sigma\rVert=\mathcal{O}(n)

initially, the complexity of one iteration remains as
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{3})

throughout. Since there are
\sqrt{n}\log R
outer iterations, the
algorithm uses a total of
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{3.5}\operatorname{polylog}R)
queries.

Extension to general logconcave distributions.

Our rounding algorithm essentially follows this approach, with several
technical refinements. First of all, we define a
ground set

of a general logconcave distribution, namely the level set
\mathsf{L}_{g}:=\{x:V(x)-\min V\leq 10n\}
.
This ground set takes up most of measure due to the universal property
in terms of the potential value (Lemma
A.6
). Focusing
on the ground set is the first step toward a streamlined extension
of the previous approach, so we consider the
grounded distribution

\nu^{X}:=\pi^{X}|_{\mathsf{L}_{g}}
. Then, for
\nu_{r}^{X}:=\nu^{X}|_{B_{r}(0)}
,
we isotropize a sequence of distributions, given by
\nu_{1}^{X}\to\nu_{\delta}^{X}\to\nu_{\delta^{2}}^{X}\to\cdots\to\nu_{D}^{X}\to\nu^{X}\to\pi^{X}

for
D=\Theta(R)
.

To analyze the outer loop, we show that for an affine map
F^{X}

between
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, if
F_{\#}^{X}\nu_{r}^{X}
is near-isotropic, then

(4F^{X})_{\#}\nu_{\delta r}^{X}
is well-rounded. Unfortunately,
a varying density of
F_{\#}^{X}\nu_{\delta r}^{X}
poses a daunting
challenge in extending the previous proof in
[
JLLV24
]

to general logconcave distributions. Nonetheless, we can resolve this
issue by working again with the exponential reduction (Lemma
4.12
).
This extension asks for a universal property that an isotropic grounded
distribution has diameter of order
\mathcal{O}(n)
, similar to isotropic
uniform distributions. We show this in Lemma
4.13
.
Transferring roundedness in the steps
\nu_{D}^{X}\to\nu^{X}
and

\nu^{X}\to\pi^{X}
is relatively straightforward by combining the
change of measures and the reverse Hölder inequality for logconcave
distributions.

For the inner loop, we can still apply the algorithm from
[
JLLV21
]

(or its streamlined version in
[
KZ24
]
) with only
minor changes to constants. First, with the logconcave sampler
\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{exp}}

whose mixing rate depends on
\lVert\operatorname{Cov}\pi^{X}\rVert
rather than
\operatorname{tr}(\operatorname{Cov}\pi^{X})
,
the complexity analysis of the inner loop extends naturally to general
logconcave distributions. Next, we note that the proofs for controlling

\lVert\Sigma_{i}\rVert
and
\operatorname{tr}\Sigma_{i}
are identical to those for
uniform distributions. The proof for the doubling of the inner radius
at each iteration is nearly the same as in the uniform case, since
the existence of a large ball due to isotropy is also a
universal

property of logconcave distributions (see Lemma
A.10
).

1.2.5
Integration

For integration, we use the stronger guarantees of our logconcave
sampler, along with a version of the
\mathsf{Tilted\ Gaussian\ Cooling}
scheme to obtain a cubic
algorithm for
well-rounded
logconcave functions. For general
logconcave functions, we use the rounding algorithm as a pre-processing
step, then apply the integration algorithm to the near-isotropic distribution
obtained after rounding.

Volume computation through annealing.

Similar to sampling, prior volume algorithms also follow a sequence

\{f_{i}\}_{i\in[m]}
of logconcave functions, moving across distributions

\mu_{i}\propto f_{i}
using a logconcave sampler. The annealing
scheme is designed in a way that
f_{1}
is easy to integrate,
\mu_{i}

is an
\mathcal{O}(1)
-warm start for
\mu_{i+1}
, the variance of the estimator

E_{i}=f_{i+1}/f_{i}
with respect to
\mu_{i}
is bounded by

m^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{i}}[E_{i}^{2}]
(i.e.,
\chi^{2}(\mu_{i+1}\mathbin{\|}\mu_{i})=\mathcal{O}(m^{-1})
),
and
f_{m+1}=f
is the target logconcave function. Since
\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{i}}E_{i}=\int f_{i+1}/\int f_{i}
,
accurate estimations of all
E_{i}
guarantee that the product
\int f_{1}\times E_{1}\cdots E_{m}

is a good estimator of
\int f_{1}\cdot\mathbb{E}E_{1}\cdots\mathbb{E}E_{m}=\int f
.

The best-known algorithm,
\mathsf{Gaussian\ Cooling}

[
CV18
]
, uses
unnormalized Gaussian densities
f_{i}(x)=\exp(-\nicefrac{{\lVert x\rVert^{2}}}{{2\sigma_{i}^{2}}})\cdot\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{K}}(x)

for a convex body
\mathcal{K}
, along with an update schedule for
\sigma_{i}^{2}
.
As described earlier in §
1.2.3
, its
uses the
\mathsf{Ball\ Walk}
to sample from
\mu_{i}\propto f_{i}
with
\mathsf{TV}
-guarantees.

Extension to general logconcave functions.

Our integration algorithm follows this approach, but once again in
the lifted space. With
\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{ann}}
used for sampling, we follow a modified
version of
\mathsf{Tilted\ Gaussian\ Cooling}
for ease of analysis, particularly for variance
control. We use
f_{i}(x,t)=\exp(-\nicefrac{{\lVert x\rVert^{2}}}{{2\sigma_{i}^{2}}}-\rho_{i}t)|_{\mathcal{K}}

as the intermediate annealing functions, and ensure that
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}(\mu_{i}\mathbin{\|}\mu_{i+1})=\mathcal{O}(1)

for efficient sampling from
\mu_{i+1}
, and
\mathcal{R}_{2}(\mu_{i+1}\mathbin{\|}\mu_{i})=\exp(\mathcal{O}(m^{-1}))

for
\operatorname{var}_{\mu_{i}}(E_{i}/\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{i}}E_{i})=\mathcal{O}(m^{-1})
. Since
\mathcal{O}(1)
-warmness
can be shown as in
\mathsf{Tilted\ Gaussian\ Cooling}
, we elaborate on technical tools for variance
control along with design of the algorithm. In Phase I, we go from

\exp(-\nicefrac{{n\lVert x\rVert^{2}}}{{2}})|_{\mathcal{K}}
to
\exp(-\nicefrac{{\lVert x\rVert^{2}}}{{2}})|_{\mathcal{K}}

with the update
\sigma^{2}\leftarrow\sigma^{2}(1+\nicefrac{{1}}{{n}})
, where
variance control is achieved by the logconcavity of
a\mapsto a^{n}\int h^{a}

for a logconcave function
h

[
KV06
]
. In Phase
II, we move from
\exp(-\nicefrac{{\lVert x\rVert^{2}}}{{2}}-t)|_{\mathcal{K}}
to
\exp(-\nicefrac{{\lVert x\rVert^{2}}}{{2}}-nt)|_{\mathcal{K}}

with the update
\rho\leftarrow\rho\,(1+\nicefrac{{1}}{{n}})
, where variance
control follows from the previous lemma and another lemma in
[
CV18
]
.
Lastly in Phase III, we move from
\exp(-\nicefrac{{\lVert x\rVert^{2}}}{{2}}-nt)|_{\mathcal{K}}

to
\exp(-nt)|_{\mathcal{K}}
with the update
\sigma^{2}\leftarrow\sigma^{2}(1+\nicefrac{{\sigma^{2}}}{{n}})
,
and we use the lemma in
[
CV18
]
again for variance
control.

Streamlined statistical analysis.

If all samples used are
independent,
and the estimators have
moderate variance, namely
\operatorname{var}_{\mu_{i}}(\nicefrac{{E_{i}}}{{\mathbb{E}E_{i}}})=\mathcal{O}(m^{-1})
,
then
\operatorname{var}(E_{1}\cdots E_{m})=(\prod(1+m^{-1})-1)\,(\mathbb{E}E_{i})^{2}\approx\mathcal{O}(1)\,(\mathbb{E}[E_{1}\cdots E_{m}])^{2}
,
which implies concentration of the estimator around
\mathbb{E}[E_{1}\cdots E_{m}]=\mathbb{E}E_{1}\cdots\mathbb{E}E_{m}

through Chebyshev’s inequality. However, samples given by (say) the

\mathsf{Ball\ Walk}
in
\mathsf{Gaussian\ Cooling}
are
approximately
distributed according to

\mu_{i}
. In fact, samples drawn from
\mu_{i}
and
\mu_{i+1}

are
dependent
, since a sample from
\mu_{i}
is used as a
warm-start for
\mu_{i+1}
. This means that the estimator
E_{1}\cdots E_{m}

is a
biased estimator
of
\mathbb{E}E_{1}\cdots\mathbb{E}E_{m}(\neq\mathbb{E}[E_{1}\cdots E_{m}])
.
These two issues complicate statistical analysis in prior work on
how close the estimator is to the integral of
f
, and require additional
technical tools to address them. For the first issue, previous work
used a coupling argument based on
\mathsf{TV}
-distance (referred to as
“divine intervention”) to account for the effects of approximate
distributions (rather than exactly
\mu_{i}
). For the second, they
used the notion of
\alpha
-mixing
[
Ros56
]

(referred to as “
\mu
-independence” therein) to bound the bias
of the product estimator.

We simplify this statistical analysis substantially by using stronger
guarantees of our sampler
\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{ann}}
. For the first issue, when
\bar{\mu}_{i}

denotes the actual law of a sample
X_{i}
satisfying
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}(\bar{\mu}_{i}\mathbin{\|}\mu_{i})\leq\varepsilon
,
we can notice that the probability of a bad event (any event) with
respect to
\bar{\mu}_{i}
(instead of
\mu_{i}
) only increases
by at most a multiplicative factor of
1+\varepsilon
. Since the mixing
time of
\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{ann}}
has a polylogarithmic dependence on
\nicefrac{{1}}{{\varepsilon}}
,
we can set
\varepsilon
polynomially small (i.e.,
\varepsilon\leftarrow\varepsilon/\operatorname{poly}(n,R)
),
so we can enforce
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}(\otimes_{i}\bar{\mu}_{i}\mathbin{\|}\otimes_{i}\mu_{i})\leq\varepsilon

without a huge overhead in the query complexity. For the second
issue, we use notion of
\beta
-mixing (or the coefficient of absolute
regularity)
[
KR60
]
, which is stronger than
\alpha
-mixing.
This quantity basically measures the discrepancy between a joint distribution
and the product of marginal distributions by
\lVert\operatorname{law}(X_{i},X_{i+1})-\operatorname{law}X_{i}\otimes\operatorname{law}X_{i+1}\rVert_{\mathsf{TV}}=\lVert\operatorname{law}(X_{i},X_{i+1})-\bar{\mu}_{i}\otimes\bar{\mu}_{i+1}\rVert_{\mathsf{TV}}\leq\beta
.
Since the mixing of
\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{ann}}
via (

LSI

) ensures mixing from

any start
(Lemma
3.5
), we can easily
bound
\beta
by
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)
(see (
5.2
) for details).
Thus, when analyzing the probability of a bad event, we can replace

\otimes\bar{\mu}_{i}
with
\operatorname{law}(X_{1},\dots,X_{m})
at the additive
cost of
\mathcal{O}(m\varepsilon)
in probability (we can replace
\varepsilon\leftarrow m\varepsilon

once again).

1.3
Preliminaries

Let
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
be the family of probability measures (distributions)
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
that are absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue
measure. We use the same symbol for a distribution and density. For
a set
S
and its indicator function
\mathds{1}_{S}(x)=[x\in S]
, we
use
\mu|_{S}
to denote a distribution
\mu
truncated to
S

(i.e.,
\mu|_{S}\propto\mu\cdot\mathds{1}_{S}
). For a measurable map
F:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}

and
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, the
pushforward
measure
F_{\#}\mu

is defined as
F_{\#}\mu(A)=(\mu\circ F^{-1})(A)
for a measurable
set
A
. For
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, we use
a\vee b
and
a\wedge b
to indicate
their maximum and minimum, respectively. We use
B_{r}^{n}(x_{0})

to denote the
n
-dimensional ball of radius
r>0
centered at
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
dropping the superscript
n
if there is no confusion. We use
a=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(b)

to denote
a=\mathcal{O}(b\operatorname{polylog}b)
. For two symmetric matrices
A,B
,
we use
\lvert A\rvert\preceq B
to denote
-B\preceq A\preceq B
. Unless
specified otherwise, for a vector
v
and a PSD matrix
M
,
\lVert v\rVert

and
\lVert\Sigma\rVert
refer to the
\ell_{2}
-norm of
v
and the
operator norm of
\Sigma
, respectively. We use
\overline{\mathbb{R}}

to denote the extended real number system
\mathbb{R}\cup\{\pm\infty\}
.

We recall notions of common probability divergences/distances between
distributions.

Definition 1.9
.

For
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, the
f
-divergence

of
\mu
towards
\nu
with
\mu\ll\nu
is defined as, for a convex
function
f:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
with
f(1)=0
and
f^{\prime}(\infty)=\infty
,

D_{f}(\mu\mathbin{\|}\nu):=\int f\bigl(\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu}{\mathrm{d}\nu}\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\nu\,.

For
q\in(1,\infty)
, the
\mathsf{KL}
-divergence and
\chi^{q}
-divergence

correspond to
f(x)=x\log x
and
x^{q}-1
, respectively. The
q
-Rényi
divergence
is defined as

\mathcal{R}_{q}(\mu\mathbin{\|}\nu):=\tfrac{1}{q-1}\log\bigl(\chi^{q}(\mu\mathbin{\|}\nu)+1\bigr)\,.

The
Rényi-infinity divergence
is defined as

\mathcal{R}_{\infty}(\mu\mathbin{\|}\nu):=\log\operatorname{ess\,sup}_{\mu}\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu}{\mathrm{d}\nu}\,.

A distribution
\mu
is said to be
M
-warm with respect to
a distribution

\nu
if
\frac{\mu(S)}{\nu(S)}\leq M
for any measurable
subset
S
(i.e.,
\mu
is
\exp(\mathcal{R}_{\infty}(\mu\mathbin{\|}\nu))
-warm

with respect to
\nu
). The
total variation
(TV) distance
for
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is defined by

\lVert\mu-\nu\rVert_{\mathsf{TV}}:=\frac{1}{2}\int|\mu(x)-\nu(x)|\,\mathrm{d}x=\sup_{S\in\mathcal{F}}\lvert\mu(S)-\nu(S)\rvert\,,

where
\mathcal{F}
is the collection of all measurable subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

We recall
\mathsf{KL}=\lim_{q\downarrow 1}\mathcal{R}_{q}\leq\mathcal{R}_{q}\leq\mathcal{R}_{q^{\prime}}\leq\mathcal{R}_{\infty}

for
1\leq q\leq q^{\prime}
and
2\,\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathsf{TV}}^{2}\leq\mathsf{KL}\leq\mathcal{R}_{2}=\log(\chi^{2}+1)\leq\chi^{2}
.
We refer readers to
[
VEH14
]
for basic properties of the
Rényi-divergence (e.g., continuity/monotonicity in
q
). The data
processing inequality is given in Lemma
A.3
for reference.

2
Sampling from logconcave distributions

In this section, we study the complexity of sampling from a logconcave
distribution under the setup stated in the introduction. We remark
that the “roundness” (or regularity) of the distribution is specified
through the existence of a ball in a level set of
constant
measure
. We find it more convenient to work with the
values
of the potential
(see Lemma
2.6
). More
precisely, we will focus on a particular level set, called the
ground
set
of
\pi
, defined as

\mathsf{L}_{\pi,g}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:V(x)-\min V\leq 10n\}\,.

(2.1)

Note that the ground set of
\pi
is
independent
of the normalization
constant of
\pi
, which justifies using
\pi
in the subscript
to indicate its uniqueness. We use
\mathsf{L}_{V,g}
to reveal a specific
choice of the potential
V
. We also define the notation:
V_{0}:=\min V
,
and a level set
\mathsf{L}_{\pi,l}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:V(x)-\min V\leq nl\}
.

Hereafter, in
\mathsf{Eval}_{\mathcal{P}}(V)
, the requirement of inclusion
of
B_{1}(x_{0})
in the level set of
\pi
of measure
1/8
is
replaced by
B_{1}(x_{0})\subset\mathsf{L}_{\pi^{X},g}
.
As we will
see in Lemma
2.6
, this is a weaker condition.

Problem 2.1

(Zeroth-order logconcave sampling from a warm start)
.

Assume access to a well-defined function oracle
\mathsf{Eval}(V)
for convex

V:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}
. Given target accuracy
\varepsilon>0
and
M
-warm
initial distribution for
\pi^{X}
, how many evaluation queries do
we need to obtain a sample
X_{*}
such that

D(\operatorname{law}X_{*},\pi^{X})\leq\varepsilon\quad\text{for }D=\mathsf{TV},\mathsf{KL},\mathcal{R}_{q},\text{ etc.}

Note that this
does not
require the knowledge of
x_{0},R
,
and
\min V
. Also, if the inner radius is
r
, then we can simply
rescale the whole system by
x\mapsto r^{-1}x
.

We prove
Result 1
in this section:

Theorem 2.2

(Restatement of Theorem
1.5
)
.

In the setting
of Problem
2.1
, there exists an algorithm that
for given
\varepsilon,\eta\in(0,1)
,
q\in[2,\infty)
, and
M
-warm
initial distribution for
\pi^{X}
, with probability at least
1-\eta

returns a sample
X_{*}
satisfying
\mathcal{R}_{q}(\operatorname{law}X_{*}\mathbin{\|}\pi^{X})\leq\varepsilon
,
using
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(qMn^{2}(\lVert\operatorname{Cov}\pi^{X}\rVert\vee 1)\log^{4}\nicefrac{{1}}{{\eta\varepsilon}})

evaluation queries in expectation.

See Theorem
2.15
for details. When
\lVert\operatorname{Cov}\pi^{X}\rVert=\Omega(1)

(the primary regime for algorithmic applications later), this complexity
bound generalizes the well-known complexity bound of
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(qMn^{2}\lVert\operatorname{Cov}\pi^{X}\rVert\operatorname{polylog}\nicefrac{{1}}{{\eta\varepsilon}})

for uniform sampling from a convex body
[
KVZ24
]
. Also,
if the inner radius is
r
, then the query complexity includes an
additional multiplicative factor of
r^{-2}
.

2.1
Reduction to an exponential distribution

The logconcave sampling problem can be reduced to the following exponential
sampling problem:

\mathrm{d}\pi(x,t)\propto\exp(-nt)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\quad\text{subject to }\mathcal{K}:=\bigl\{(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}:V(x)\leq nt\bigr\}\,.

(exp-red)

We note that convexity of the constraint
\mathcal{K}
follows from that of

V
, and that the
X
-marginal of
\pi
exactly matches the desired
target
\pi^{X}\propto\exp(-V)
.

Proposition 2.3
.

The
X
-marginal
\pi^{X}
of
\pi
is
proportional to
\exp(-V)
.

Proof.

We just integrate out
t
as follows:

\pi^{X}(x)=\int\pi(x,t)\,\mathrm{d}t=\frac{\int\exp(-nt)|_{[V(x)\leq nt]}\,\mathrm{d}t}{\int\exp(-nt)|_{[V(x)\leq nt]}\,\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}x}=\frac{\exp\bigl(-V(x)\bigr)}{\int\exp\bigl(-V(x)\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}x}\,,

where the last equality follows from

\int\exp(-nt)|_{[V(x)\leq nt]}\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{V(x)/n}^{\infty}\exp(-nt)\,\mathrm{d}t=\frac{1}{n}\int_{V(x)}^{\infty}\exp(-u)\,\mathrm{d}u=\frac{1}{n}\exp\bigl(-V(x)\bigr)\,,

which completes the proof.
∎

This implies that in order to sample from
\exp(-V)
, one can just
focus on sampling
(x,t)\sim\pi
and take
x
-part only. Precisely,
once we obtain a sample
(X,T)\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
such that
D\bigl(\operatorname{law}(X,T)\mathbin{\|}\pi\bigr)\leq\varepsilon

for the
f
-divergence or Rényi divergence
D
, the data-processing
inequality (DPI) (Lemma
A.3
) ensures that
D\bigl(\operatorname{law}X\mathbin{\|}\pi^{X}\bigr)\leq\varepsilon
.
Therefore, the general logconcave sampling problem can be reduced
to sampling from a particular form of the exponential distribution
above. While it allows us to move to the simpler problem, for its
mixing analysis, we will need to know the mean and variance along
the new dimension as well as the operator norm of the covariance matrix
of
\pi
.

Mean.

For the mean
(\mu_{x},\mu_{t}):=\mathbb{E}_{\pi}[(X,T)]\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
,
we establish a useful bound on
\mu_{t}=\mathbb{E}_{\pi}T
.

Lemma 2.4
.

In the setting of Problem
2.1
,
it holds that
0\leq\mu_{t}-V_{0}/n\leq\sqrt{10}
.

Proof.

We directly compute
\mu_{t}=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{T}}T=\mathbb{E}_{\pi}T
as follows:

\displaystyle\mu_{t}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi}T=\frac{\int te^{-nt}|_{[V(x)\leq nt]}\,\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}x}{\int e^{-nt}|_{[V(x)\leq nt]}\,\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}x}=\frac{\int\int_{V(x)/n}^{\infty}te^{-nt}\,\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}x}{n^{-1}\int\exp(-V)\,\mathrm{d}x}=\frac{\int\int_{V(x)}^{\infty}ue^{-u}\,\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}x}{n\int\exp(-V)\,\mathrm{d}x}

\displaystyle=\frac{\int\bigl([-ue^{-u}]_{V(x)}^{\infty}+\int_{V(x)}^{\infty}e^{-u}\,\mathrm{d}u\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}x}{n\int\exp(-V)\,\mathrm{d}x}=\frac{\int(V+1)\,\exp(-V)\,\mathrm{d}x}{n\int\exp(-V)\,\mathrm{d}x}

\displaystyle=n^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{X}}(V+1)=n^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}(V+1)\,.

We now prove
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}(V-V_{0})\leq\sqrt{10}n
. For
\pi^{X}=\exp(-W)/\int\exp(-W)

with
W:=V-V_{0}\geq 0
, Lemma
A.6
ensures that for

\beta\geq 2
,

\mathbb{P}_{\pi}\bigl(W(X)\geq\beta\,(n-1)\bigr)\leq(e^{1-\beta}\beta)^{n-1}\,.

Therefore, for
n\geq 2
and the Gamma function
\Gamma
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi}W

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\pi}\bigl(W(X)\geq u\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{2(n-1)}\mathbb{P}_{\pi}\bigl(W(X)\geq u\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}u+\int_{2(n-1)}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\pi}\bigl(W(X)\geq u\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle\leq 2\,(n-1)+(n-1)\int_{2}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\pi}\bigl(W(X)\geq\beta\,(n-1)\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\beta

\displaystyle\leq 2\,(n-1)+(n-1)\int_{0}^{\infty}(e^{1-\beta}\beta)^{n-1}\,\mathrm{d}\beta\,

\displaystyle\leq n\,\Bigl(2\,\frac{n-1}{n}+\bigl(\frac{e}{n-1}\bigr)^{n-1}\frac{\Gamma(n)}{n}\Bigr)\,.

When
n=1
, by using
\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(W(X)\geq t)\leq e^{-t}
by
[
LV07
, Lemma 5.6(a)]
,
we also have
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}W\leq 1
. One can manually check that the final
bound is at most
\sqrt{10}n
for
n\leq 7
. When
n\geq 8
, since
the digamma function
\psi(x):=\log\Gamma(x)
satisfies
\psi(x)\leq\log x
,
the second term in the parenthesis is decreasing in
n
, and thus

\mathbb{E}_{\pi}W\leq\sqrt{10}n
as well. Substituting this bound back to
the expression of
\mu_{t}
, it follows that

\frac{V_{0}}{n}\leq\mu_{t}\leq\frac{V_{0}}{n}+\sqrt{10}\,,

which completes the proof.
∎

Covariance.

Let us denote the covariance of
\pi,\pi^{X},\pi^{T}
by
\Sigma:=\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\bigl[\bigl((X,T)-(\mu_{x},\mu_{t})\bigr)^{\otimes 2}\bigr]
,

\Sigma_{x}:=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{X}}[(X-\mu_{x})^{\otimes 2}]
,
\Sigma_{t}:=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{T}}[(T-\mu_{t})^{2}]
,
respectively. We study two important quantities here — the largest
eigenvalue (i.e., operator norm of
\Sigma
) and trace (i.e., second
moment of
\pi
).

Lemma 2.5
.

The variance in the
t
-direction is at most

160
. Moreover,
\operatorname{tr}\Sigma\leq\operatorname{tr}\Sigma_{x}+160
, and
\lVert\Sigma\rVert\leq 2\,(\lVert\Sigma_{x}\rVert+160)
.

Proof.

Let us bound
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}[\lvert T-\mu_{t}\rvert^{2}]=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{T}}[\lvert T-\mu_{t}\rvert^{2}]
.
By the reverse Hölder (Lemma
A.9
),

\mathbb{E}_{\pi}[\lvert T-\mu_{t}\rvert^{2}]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\pi}[\lvert T-V_{0}/n\rvert^{2}]\leq 16\,(\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\lvert T-V_{0}/n\rvert)^{2}\leq 160\,.

Hence,

\operatorname{tr}\Sigma=\mathbb{E}_{\pi}[\lVert X-\mu_{x}\rVert^{2}+\lvert T-\mu_{t}\rvert^{2}]=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{X}}[\lVert X-\mu_{x}\rVert^{2}]+\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{T}}[\lvert T-\mu_{t}\rvert^{2}]\leq R^{2}+160\,.

As for the largest eigenvalue, it follows that for a unit vector
v=(v_{x},v_{t})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
,

\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\bigl[\bigl(v_{x}\cdot(X-\mu_{x})+v_{t}\,(T-\mu_{t})\bigr)^{2}\bigr]\leq 2\,\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\bigl[\bigl(v_{x}\cdot(X-\mu_{x})\bigr)^{2}\bigr]+2\,\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\bigl[\bigl(v_{t}\,(T-\mu_{t})\bigr)^{2}\bigr]\leq 2\,(\lVert\Sigma_{x}\rVert+160)\,.

Hence,
\lVert\Sigma\rVert\leq 2\,(\lVert\Sigma_{x}\rVert+160)
.
∎

We now show that the ‘measure-based’ regularity implies the ‘value-based’
one.

Lemma 2.6
.

When a level set of
\pi^{X}\propto\exp(-V)

of measure
\mathsf{m}<1
contains
B_{r}(x_{0})
for
r>0
and
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
the level set
\mathsf{L}_{V,l}=\{V(x)-V_{0}\leq\ell n\}
with
\ell=5+13\log\frac{e}{1-\mathsf{m}}

contains
B_{r}(x_{0})
.

Proof.

Consider the exponential reduction
\pi(x,t)\propto\exp(-nt)|_{\mathcal{K}}

for
\mathcal{K}=\{(x,t):V(x)\leq nt\}
. Since the variance in the
t
-direction
is at most
160
, Lemma
A.7
leads to

\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(\lvert T-\mu_{t}\rvert\geq 13c)=\mathbb{P}_{\pi^{T}}(\lvert T-\mu_{t}\rvert\geq 13c)\leq\exp(-c+1)\quad\text{for any }c>0\,,

which implies
\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(T\geq 5+V_{0}/n+13c)\leq\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(T\geq\mu_{t}+13c)\leq\exp(-c+1)
.
Hence, for
c=-\log\frac{e}{1-\mathsf{m}}
, we have
\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(T-V_{0}/n\leq\ell)\geq\mathsf{m}
.
Since
\mathcal{K}\cap[t\leq V_{0}/n+\ell]=\mathcal{K}\cap(\mathsf{L}_{V,l}\times\mathbb{R})
,
it follows from
\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(T\leq V_{0}/n+\ell)=\pi^{X}(\mathsf{L}_{V,l})

that
\mathsf{L}_{V,l}
contains
B_{r}(x_{0})
.
∎

2.2
Proximal sampler for the reduced exponential distribution

We now propose a sampling algorithm for this exponential distribution.
For
z:=(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
and parameter
h>0
, we use the
\mathsf{Proximal\ Sampler}

(
\mathsf{PS}
) to sample from

\pi^{Z,Y}(z,y)\propto\exp\bigl(-\alpha^{\mathsf{T}}z-\frac{1}{2h}\,\lVert z-y\rVert^{2}\bigr)|_{\mathcal{K}}\quad\text{for }\alpha=ne_{n+1}\,,

where its
Z
-marginal
\pi^{Z}
corresponds to our desired target

\pi^{Z}\propto\exp(-\alpha^{\mathsf{T}}z)|_{\mathcal{K}}
.

The
\mathsf{Proximal\ Sampler}
for this target, called
\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{exp}}
(Algorithm
1
),
repeats the following two steps:

•

[Forward]
y\sim\pi^{Y|Z=z}=\mathcal{N}(z,hI_{n+1})
.

•

[Backward]
z\sim\pi^{Z|Y=y}\propto\exp(-\alpha^{\mathsf{T}}z-\frac{1}{2h}\,\lVert y-z\rVert^{2})|_{\mathcal{K}}=\mathcal{N}(y-h\alpha,hI_{n+1})|_{\mathcal{K}}

for
\alpha=ne_{n+1}
,

and the backward step is implemented by rejection sampling with proposal

\mathcal{N}(y-h\alpha,hI_{n+1})
.

As in previous work, the analysis can be divided into two parts: (1)
mixing towards the target (§
2.2.1
), and (2) the
query complexity of implementing the backward step (§
2.2.2
).
The first part can be analyzed similarly to prior work on uniform
sampling
[
KVZ24
]
or constrained Gaussian sampling
[
KZ25
]
,
while the second part introduces additional technical challenges.

2.2.1
Convergence rate

One iteration of the
\mathsf{Proximal\ Sampler}
(one forward step
+
one backward step)
for
\pi^{X}\propto\exp(-V)
can be viewed as a composition of forward
and backward heat flow, where it can be written as follows: for
\pi(x,y)\propto\exp\bigl(-V(x)-\frac{1}{2h}\lVert x-y\rVert^{2}\bigr)
,
repeat (i)
y_{i+1}\sim\pi^{Y|X=x_{i}}=\mathcal{N}(x_{i},hI_{n})
and (ii)

x_{i+1}\sim\pi^{X|Y=y_{i+1}}
. To make this perspective concrete,
we borrow expositions from
[
KVZ24
, p.4-5]
and
[
Che24
, §8.3]
.
For the laws of
x_{i}
and
y_{i}
denoted by
\mu_{i}^{X}
and

\mu_{i}^{Y}
, the forward step can be described as
\mu_{i+1}^{Y}=\int\pi^{Y|X=x}\,\mu_{i}^{X}(\mathrm{d}x)=\mu_{i}^{X}*\mathcal{N}(0,hI_{n})
.
This step can be seen as simulating a Brownian motion with initial
measure
\mu_{i}^{X}
for time
h
:

\mathrm{d}Z_{t}=\mathrm{d}B_{t}\quad\text{with }Z_{0}\sim\mu_{i}^{X}\Longrightarrow Z_{h}\sim\mu_{i+1}^{Y}\,.

Let us denote
\mu P_{t}:=\mu*\mathcal{N}(0,tI_{n})
for
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

(i.e.,
(P_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is the heat semigroup). Next, the backward
step corresponds to
\mu_{i+1}^{X}=\int\pi^{X|Y=y}\,\mu_{i+1}^{Y}(\mathrm{d}y)
,
and it turns out that this process can also be represented by an SDE
(called the backward heat flow): for a new Brownian motion
B_{t}
,

\mathrm{d}Z_{t}^{\leftarrow}=\nabla\log(\pi^{X}P_{h-t})(Z_{t}^{\leftarrow})\,\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}B_{t}\quad\text{for }t\in[0,h]\,.

Its construction ensures that if
Z_{0}^{\leftarrow}\sim\delta_{y}

(a point mass at
y
), then
Z_{h}^{\leftarrow}\sim\pi^{X|Y=y}
.
Hence, if we initialize this SDE with
Z_{0}^{\leftarrow}\sim\mu_{i+1}^{Y}
,
then the law of
Z_{h}^{\leftarrow}
corresponds to
\int\pi^{X|Y=y}\,\mu_{i+1}^{Y}(\mathrm{d}y)=\mu_{i+1}^{X}
.

This perspective establishes a natural connection between mixing guarantees
and constants of functional inequalities (e.g., (

PI

) and
(

LSI

)) for a target distribution.
[
CCSW22
]

has already demonstrated this connection for the
\mathsf{Proximal\ Sampler}
in the case
of smooth, unconstrained distributions.

Proposition 2.7

(
[
CCSW22
, Theorem 3 and 4]
)
.

Assume that
\pi\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
has a smooth density, and let
P

denote the Markov kernel of the
\mathsf{Proximal\ Sampler}
. If
\pi
satisfies (

LSI

)
with constant
C_{\mathsf{LSI}}
, then for any
q\geq 1
and distribution
\mu

with smooth density and
\mu\ll\pi
,

\mathcal{R}_{q}(\mu P\mathbin{\|}\pi)\leq\frac{\mathcal{R}_{q}(\mu\mathbin{\|}\pi)}{(1+h/C_{\mathsf{LSI}})^{2/q}}\,.

(2.2)

If
\pi
satisfies (

PI

) with constant
C_{\mathsf{PI}}
, then

\chi^{2}(\mu P\mathbin{\|}\pi)\leq\frac{\chi^{2}(\mu\mathbin{\|}\pi)}{(1+h/C_{\mathsf{PI}})^{2}}\,.

(2.3)

Moreover, for all
q\geq 2
,

\mathcal{R}_{q}(\mu P\mathbin{\|}\pi)\leq\begin{cases}\mathcal{R}_{q}(\mu\mathbin{\|}\pi)-\frac{2\log(1+h/C_{\mathsf{PI}})}{q}&\text{if }\mathcal{R}_{q}(\mu\mathbin{\|}\pi)\geq 1\,,\\
\frac{\mathcal{R}_{q}(\mu\mathbin{\|}\pi)}{(1+h/C_{\mathsf{PI}})^{2/q}}&\text{if }\mathcal{R}_{q}(\mu\mathbin{\|}\pi)\leq 1\,.\end{cases}

(2.4)

Hence, its mixing times are roughly
qh^{-1}C_{\mathsf{LSI}}\log\frac{\log\mathcal{R}_{q}}{\varepsilon}

and
qh^{-1}C_{\mathsf{PI}}\log\frac{\mathcal{R}_{q}}{\varepsilon}
under (

LSI

)
and (

PI

), respectively. We can extend this mixing result
to constrained distributions under mild assumptions.

Lemma 2.8

(
[
KVZ24
, Lemma 22]
)
.

Let

\pi
be a probability measure, absolutely continuous with respect
to the Lebesgue measure over
\mathcal{K}
. For
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
the
heat semigroup, the forward and backward heat flow equations given
by

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mu_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,\Delta\mu_{t}\,,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mu_{t}^{\leftarrow}

\displaystyle=-\operatorname{div}\bigl(\mu_{t}^{\leftarrow}\nabla\log(\pi P_{h-t})\bigr)+\frac{1}{2}\,\Delta\mu_{t}^{\leftarrow}\quad\text{with }\mu_{0}^{\leftarrow}=\mu_{h}\,,

admit solutions on
(0,h]
, and the weak limit
\lim_{t\to h}\mu_{t}^{\leftarrow}=\mu_{h}^{\leftarrow}

exists for any initial measure
\mu_{0}
with support
3
3

3

The original result in
[
KVZ24
]
is proven only for a
bounded support, but it can be extended to an unbounded support since
the almost sure pointwise convergence of
\pi*\mathcal{N}(0,\varepsilon I_{n})

to
\pi
holds as
\varepsilon\to 0

[
Fol99
, Theorem 8.15]
.
contained in
\operatorname{supp}\pi
. Moreover, for any
q
-Rényi divergence
with
q\in(1,\infty)
,

\mathcal{R}_{q}(\mu_{h}^{\leftarrow}\mathbin{\|}\pi)\leq\lim_{t\downarrow 0}\mathcal{R}_{q}(\mu_{h-t}^{\leftarrow}\mathbin{\|}\pi P_{t})\,.

Using this lemma, we now establish a mixing of the
\mathsf{Proximal\ Sampler}
(for potentially
constrained distributions) in
\chi^{2}
or
\mathcal{R}_{q}
for any

q\geq 2
under (

PI

), and in
\mathcal{R}_{q}
for any
q\geq 1

under (

LSI

).

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
P
be the Markov kernel of the
\mathsf{Proximal\ Sampler}

and
\mu
be any initial distribution supported on
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Then, the contractions (
2.2
) under (

LSI

),
and (
2.3
)-(
2.4
) under (

PI

)
remain valid for
C_{\mathsf{LSI}}=C_{\mathsf{LSI}}(\pi^{X})
and
C_{\mathsf{PI}}=C_{\mathsf{PI}}(\pi^{X})
.

Proof.

For any
\epsilon>0
, we have that
\mu_{\epsilon}=\mu*\mathcal{N}(0,\epsilon I_{n})

and
\pi_{\epsilon}=\pi^{X}*\mathcal{N}(0,\epsilon I_{n})
are
C^{\infty}
-smooth.
Let
D
denote either
\chi^{2}
or
\mathcal{R}_{q}
, and define
C_{\epsilon}:=1+\tfrac{h-\epsilon}{C_{*}(\pi^{X})+\epsilon}\leq 1+\tfrac{h-\epsilon}{C_{*}(\pi_{\epsilon})}

for
*\in\{\mathsf{PI},\mathsf{LSI}\}
, where the last inequality holds
due to
[
Cha04
, Corollary 13]
:

C_{\mathsf{PI}}\bigl(\pi^{X}*\mathcal{N}(0,\epsilon I_{n})\bigr)\leq C_{\mathsf{PI}}(\pi^{X})+\epsilon\quad\&\quad C_{\mathsf{LSI}}\bigl(\pi^{X}*\mathcal{N}(0,\epsilon I_{n})\bigr)\leq C_{\mathsf{LSI}}(\pi^{X})+\epsilon\,.

Hence, by Proposition
2.7
with step size
h-\epsilon
,
we obtain that if
\mathcal{R}_{q}(\mu\mathbin{\|}\pi^{X})\leq 1
, for
\mu_{0}=\mu
,

D(\mu_{h-\epsilon}^{\leftarrow}\mathbin{\|}\pi_{\epsilon})\leq C_{\epsilon}^{-1}D(\mu_{\epsilon}\mathbin{\|}\pi_{\epsilon})\leq C_{\epsilon}^{-1}D(\mu\mathbin{\|}\pi^{X})\,,

where the last inequality follows from the DPI (Lemma
A.3
)
for the
f
-divergence (
\chi^{2}
) and Rényi divergence
\mathcal{R}_{q}
.
By the lower semicontinuity of
D
(Lemma
2.8
),
sending
\epsilon\to 0
leads to

D(\mu P\mathbin{\|}\pi^{X})=D(\mu_{h}^{\leftarrow}\mathbin{\|}\pi^{X})\leq C_{0}^{-1}D(\mu\mathbin{\|}\pi^{X})=\frac{\chi^{2}(\mu\mathbin{\|}\pi^{X})}{\bigl(1+h/C_{\mathsf{PI}}(\pi^{X})\bigr)^{2}}\ \text{ or }\ \frac{\mathcal{R}_{q}(\mu\mathbin{\|}\pi^{X})}{\bigl(1+h/C_{\mathsf{PI}}(\pi^{X})\bigr)^{2/q}}\,.

When
\mathcal{R}_{q}(\mu\mathbin{\|}\pi^{X})\geq 1
, a similar limiting argument
shows that

\mathcal{R}_{q}(\mu P\mathbin{\|}\pi^{X})\leq\mathcal{R}_{q}(\mu_{\epsilon}\mathbin{\|}\pi_{\epsilon})-\frac{2\log C_{\epsilon}}{q}\leq\mathcal{R}_{q}(\mu\mathbin{\|}\pi^{X})-\frac{2\log C_{0}}{q}\,,

where in the first inequality we used the lower-semicontinuity of

\mathcal{R}_{q}
, and in the second inequality used the DPI and then sent

\epsilon\to 0
. Contraction in
\mathcal{R}_{q}
for any
q\geq 1
under
(

LSI

) follows from the same argument above.
∎

We can simply set
X\leftarrow Z=(X,T)
and
Y\leftarrow Y
above to obtain
a mixing guarantee of the
\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{exp}}
in terms of
C_{\mathsf{PI}}(\pi^{Z})
,
which satisfies
C_{\mathsf{PI}}(\pi^{Z})\lesssim(C_{\mathsf{PI}}(\pi^{X})+1)\log n
due
to
[
Kla23
]
and Lemma
2.5
.

Remark 2.10
.

In general, one cannot expect a mixing
result of the
\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{exp}}
based on (

LSI

) even if the original
distribution
\pi^{X}\propto\exp(-V)
satisfies the LSI, as it does
not hold in general for an exponential distribution. Hence, before
sampling, an additional step such as truncation to a bounded convex
set may be required. We will go through this procedure in §
3

in order to invoke a mixing result via the LSI (see, for instance,
Lemma
3.6
).

2.2.2
Complexity of implementing the backward step

As opposed to the forward step, it is unclear how to sample from the
backward distribution
\pi^{Z|Y=y}\propto\exp(-\alpha^{\mathsf{T}}z-\frac{1}{2h}\,\lVert y-z\rVert^{2})|_{\mathcal{K}}

for
\alpha=ne_{n+1}
. Due to

\alpha^{\mathsf{T}}z+\frac{1}{2h}\,\lVert z-y\rVert^{2}=\frac{1}{2h}\,\lVert z-(y-h\alpha)\rVert^{2}+\alpha^{\mathsf{T}}y-\frac{h}{2}\,\lVert\alpha\rVert^{2}\,,

the distribution can be written as

\pi^{Z|Y=y}\propto\exp\bigl(-\frac{1}{2h}\,\lVert z-(y-h\alpha)\rVert^{2}\bigr)\big|_{\mathcal{K}}\,.

Thus, we use rejection sampling with proposal
\mathcal{N}(y-h\alpha,hI_{n+1})
,
accepting a proposed sample only if it lies within
\mathcal{K}
(which can
be checked by using the evaluation oracle). The expected number of
trials until the first acceptance is

\frac{1}{\ell(y)}:=\frac{(2\pi h)^{(n+1)/2}}{\int_{\mathcal{K}}\exp(-\frac{1}{2h}\,\lVert z-(y-h\alpha)\rVert^{2})\,\mathrm{d}z}\,.

We show in this section that the expected number of wasted proposals
is moderate under suitable choices of variance
h
and threshold

N
in the
\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{exp}}
. To this end, we first establish analytical
and geometrical properties of
\pi^{Y}=\pi^{Z}*\mathcal{N}(0,hI_{n+1})
.

Density after taking the forward heat flow.

We deduce a density of
\pi^{Y}=\pi^{Z}*\mathcal{N}(0,hI_{n+1})
, using
the definition of convolution.

Lemma 2.11

(Density of
\pi^{Y}
)
.

For
\pi^{Z}\propto\exp(-\alpha^{\mathsf{T}}z)|_{\mathcal{K}}
,
the density of
\pi^{Y}=\pi^{Z}*\mathcal{N}(0,hI_{n+1})
is

\pi^{Y}(y)=\frac{\int_{\mathcal{K}}\exp(-\frac{1}{2h}\,\lVert z-(y-h\alpha)\rVert^{2})\,\mathrm{d}z}{\int_{\mathcal{K}}\exp(-\alpha^{\mathsf{T}}z)\,\mathrm{d}z\cdot(2\pi h)^{(n+1)/2}}\,\exp\bigl(-\alpha^{\mathsf{T}}y+\frac{1}{2}\,h\lVert\alpha\rVert^{2}\bigr)=\frac{\ell(y)\exp(-\alpha^{\mathsf{T}}y+\frac{1}{2}\,h\lVert\alpha\rVert^{2})}{\int_{\mathcal{K}}\exp(-\alpha^{\mathsf{T}}z)\,\mathrm{d}z}\,.

Proof.

By the definition of the convolution,
