\lvert\overline{\Sigma}-\Sigma\rvert\preceq\varepsilon\Sigma+\delta I_{n}\quad\text{for }\Sigma=\operatorname{Cov}\mu\,,

so long as
k\asymp\frac{\operatorname{tr}\Sigma}{\varepsilon\delta}\log^{2}\frac{n\max(n,\operatorname{tr}\Sigma)}{\delta}\log^{2}n
.

We also rely on another result to justify the covariance estimation
in Line
9
, when computing the mean
\overline{\mathsf{m}}

and covariance
\overline{\Sigma}
of
\mu_{\text{last}}
.

Lemma 4.6

(
[
KZ24
, Corollary 10 and 33]
)
.

In the setting of Lemma
4.5
,
\overline{\Sigma}

satisfies that for any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, with probability at least

1-\mathcal{O}(n^{-1})
,

\lvert\overline{\Sigma}-\Sigma\rvert\preceq\varepsilon\Sigma\quad\text{and}\quad\lVert\overline{\mathsf{m}}-\mathbb{E}_{\mu}Z\rVert^{2}\leq\varepsilon^{2}\lVert\Sigma\rVert\,,

so long as
k\asymp\frac{n}{\varepsilon^{2}}\log^{2}\frac{n}{\varepsilon}\log^{2}n
.

We note that even when the chain starts from a non-stationary distribution

\nu
, these statistical results still hold, with the bad probability
increasing by a multiplicative factor of
\exp(\mathcal{R}_{2}(\nu\mathbin{\|}\mu))=1+\chi^{2}(\nu\mathbin{\|}\mu)

(see
[
KZ24
, Lemma 29]
). As
\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{exp}}
starts from
an
\mathcal{O}(1)
-warm start and
\mathcal{R}_{2}\leq\mathcal{R}_{\infty}
, the bad
probability only increases by a factor of
\mathcal{O}(1)
.

Analysis.

We now examine the two key properties: (
\mathsf{P1}
) the largest eigenvalue
of the covariance of
\mu_{i}^{X}
increases by at most
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n)
,
and (
\mathsf{P2}
)
r_{i}=\operatorname{inrad}\mu_{i}^{X}
nearly doubles every
inner loop.
[
JLLV21
]
established these results regarding
a rounding algorithm for the
uniform distribution
over a convex
body. Notably, the proofs therein are independent of target distributions,
allowing these results to hold for logconcave distributions as well.
Here, we present an outline of these proofs, along with pointers to
detailed arguments in previous works, following a streamlined analysis
in
[
KZ24
, §4.1]
.

We first state that
\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{exp}}
employed in Algorithm
4

has a spectral gap at least
0.99
. Its proof is obvious from the
discussion in the paragraph above.

Proposition 4.7

(
[
KZ24
, Lemma 14]
)
.

In Line
4
and
9
,

\mathsf{PS}_{N_{i}}(\mu_{i},\cdot,h_{i})
with
N_{i}\asymp r_{i}^{-2}n^{3}\log n

and
h_{i}\asymp r_{i}^{2}(n^{2}\log(r_{i}n))^{-1}
has a spectral
gap at least
0.99
.

As for (
\mathsf{P1}
), we need the following quantitative bounds on
the operator norm and trace of
\Sigma_{i}^{X}=\operatorname{Cov}\mu_{i}^{X}
in
each while loop of Algorithm
4
. The original
result was established by
[
JLLV21
, Lemma 3.2]
, with
a simpler proof shown in
[
KZ24
, Lemma 16]
.

Lemma 4.8
.

In Algorithm
4
,
the while-loop iterates at most
\mathcal{O}(\log n)
times, during which

\operatorname{tr}\Sigma_{i}^{X}\lesssim r_{i}^{2}n
and
\lVert\Sigma_{i}^{X}\rVert\lesssim n(1+i)
.

The base case
i=1
holds since
\lVert\Sigma_{1}^{X}\rVert\leq\operatorname{tr}\Sigma_{1}^{X}\leq R^{2}=\mathcal{O}(n)
.
Assuming the results hold for
i
as the induction hypothesis, one
must need a guarantee on how close the covariance estimation in Line
5

is to the true covariance, in order to establish the claim for
i+1
.
The following lemma directly follows from Lemma
4.5

and Proposition
4.7
.

Proposition 4.9

(
[
KZ24
, Lemma 15]
)
.

Each while-loop ensures that with probability at least
1-\mathcal{O}(n^{-1})
,
for
\Sigma_{i}^{X}=\operatorname{Cov}\mu_{i}^{X}
and
\Sigma_{i}=\operatorname{Cov}\mu_{i}
,

\frac{9}{10}\,\Sigma_{i}^{X}-\frac{n+1}{100}\,I_{n+1}\preceq\overline{\Sigma}_{i}\preceq\frac{11}{10}\,\Sigma_{i}^{X}+\frac{n+1}{100}\,I_{n+1}\quad\text{ if}\ k_{i}\gtrsim\frac{\operatorname{tr}\Sigma_{i}}{n}\operatorname{polylog}n\,.

Since
\operatorname{tr}\Sigma_{i}\leq\operatorname{tr}\Sigma_{i}^{X}+\mathcal{O}(1)
by Lemma
2.5

and
\operatorname{tr}\Sigma_{i}^{X}\lesssim r_{i}^{2}n
from the induction hypothesis,
the result above holds when
k_{i}\gtrsim r_{i}^{2}\operatorname{polylog}n
, thereby
justifying the choice of
k_{i}
in Line
4
.
With this statistical estimation, the proof of Lemma
4.8

for
i+1
is exactly the same with
[
KZ24
, Lemma 16]
.

Regarding (
\mathsf{P2}
), we need minor adjustments to some constants
in the proof of
[
KZ24
, Lemma 17]
, whose original
version (for the uniform distribution) appeared in
[
JLLV21
, Lemma 3.2]
.

Lemma 4.10
.

In Algorithm
4
, under
r_{i+1}=2(1-\nicefrac{{1}}{{\log n}})r_{i}
,
each while-loop ensures
r_{i+1}\leq\operatorname{inrad}\mu_{i+1}^{X}
.

To demonstrate the existence of a ball of radius
r_{i+1}
centered
at some point
c_{i+1}\in\operatorname{supp}\mu_{i+1}^{X}
, the proof relies on
two ellipsoids within
\operatorname{supp}\mu_{i}^{X}
: a ball
B_{r_{i}}(c_{i})

from the induction hypothesis, and
\{\lVert x-\mathsf{m}_{i}\rVert_{(\Sigma_{i}^{X})^{-1}}\leq e^{-2}\}

for
\mathsf{m}_{i}=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{i}^{X}}X
, whose existence follows from
Lemma
A.10
. After multiplying
x\mapsto(I_{n}+P_{i})x=M_{i}x
,
for
c_{i}^{\prime}:=M_{i}c_{i}
and
\mathsf{m}_{i}^{\prime}:=M_{i}\mathsf{m}_{i}
, the
support of the pushforward
(M_{i})_{\#}\mu_{i}^{X}=\mu_{i+1}^{X}

contains two ellipsoids:

\mathcal{A}:\{(x-c_{i}^{\prime})^{\mathsf{T}}M_{i}^{-2}(x-c_{i}^{\prime})\leq r_{i}^{2}\}\,,\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{B}:\{(x-\mathsf{m}_{i}^{\prime})^{\mathsf{T}}(M_{i}\Sigma_{i}^{X}M_{i})^{-1}(x-\mathsf{m}_{i}^{\prime})\leq e^{-2}\}\,.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be the subspace spanned by the eigenvectors of
\overline{\Sigma}_{i}

with corresponding eigenvalues less than
n
. The transformation

M_{i}
scales up
\mathcal{S}
by a factor of two, ensuring that the

\mathcal{A}
-ellipsoid contains a
2r_{i}
-ball centered at
c_{i}^{\prime}

along
\mathcal{S}
. Moreover, as eigenvalues of
\overline{\Sigma}_{i}

along
S^{\perp}
are at least
n
, we can show that the
\mathcal{B}
-ellipsoid
contains an
(n^{1/2}/10)
-ball at
\mathsf{m}_{i}^{\prime}
along
\mathcal{S}^{\perp}
.
Using the convexity of
\operatorname{supp}\mu_{i+1}^{X}
, one can show that the
convex combination of these two ellipsoids leads to the existence
of a ball of radius
2(1-(\log n)^{-1})r_{i}
at some point. We refer
readers to
[
KZ24
, Lemma 17]
for details.

It is worth noting that
\mu_{i+1}^{X}\propto\exp(-W)
for a new
potential
W
satisfies that
W(x)-\min W=W(x)-\min U\leq W(x)\leq 10n

for any
x\in\operatorname{supp}\mu_{i+1}^{X}
(i.e.,
\operatorname{supp}\mu_{i+1}^{X}=\mathsf{L}_{\mu_{i+1}^{X},g}
),
and this ground set contains a ball of radius
r_{i+1}
. Given that
this aligns with the setup of Problem
2.1
, we
can proceed with the
\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{exp}}
to sample from
\mu_{i+1}^{X}
, referring
to the query complexity result in Theorem
2.15
.

After the while-loop, in Line
9
, we use

k\asymp n\operatorname{polylog}n
samples to estimate
\overline{\Sigma}
and

\overline{\mathsf{m}}
, which by Lemma
4.6

satisfies that with probability at least
1-\mathcal{O}(n^{-1})
,

(1-10^{-3})\operatorname{Cov}\mu_{\text{last}}^{X}\preceq\overline{\Sigma}\preceq(1+10^{-3})\operatorname{Cov}\mu_{\text{last}}^{X}\,.

Thus, for
F^{X}:x\mapsto\overline{\Sigma}^{-1/2}(x-\overline{\mathsf{m}})
,
it holds that
F_{\#}^{X}\mu_{\text{last}}^{X}
is
1.01
-isotropic.
Moreover, Lemma
4.6
implies that the
mean of
F_{\#}^{X}\mu_{\text{last}}^{X}
lies within a ball of radius

10^{-2}
, i.e.,

\lVert\overline{\Sigma}^{-1/2}(\mathsf{m}_{\text{last}}-\overline{\mathsf{m}})\rVert\leq 0.01\,.

Combining these results, we record the final guarantee of Algorithm
4
.
A similar result for the rounding of uniform distributions can be
found in
[
KZ24
, Lemma 18]
.

Lemma 4.11

(Guarantee of
\mathsf{Isotropize}
)
.

Assume that a distribution
\mu^{X}

satisfies Assumption
4.4
. Then, with probability
at least
1-\mathcal{O}(n^{-1/2})
, Algorithm
4

on the input
\mu
returns a distribution
F_{\#}\mu_{\textup{last}}

using
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{3})
evaluation queries such that the
X
-marginal

F_{\#}^{X}\mu_{\textup{last}}^{X}=(F_{\#}\mu_{\textup{last}})^{X}

is
1.01
-isotropic, with its mean lying within a ball of radius

0.01
.

4.2
Maintaining well-roundedness

Recall that Step 1-(b) repeats the following while
r=\mathcal{O}(R)
: if

F_{\#}^{X}\nu_{r}^{X}
is near-isotropic, then run
\mathsf{Isotropize}

on
(4F^{X})_{\#}\nu_{\delta r}^{X}
for
\delta=1+n^{-1/2}
, and
update
F^{X}\leftarrow 4F^{X}
and
r\leftarrow\delta r
. To ensure this approach
is valid, it must be the case that the input distribution
(4F^{X})_{\#}\nu_{\delta r}^{X}

satisfies Assumption
4.4
as well. We show that

[
JLLV24
, Lemma 3.4]
, proven for the
uniform distribution over a convex body, remains valid for any logconcave
distribution.

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
\delta=1+n^{-1/2}
and
F^{X}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}

an invertible affine map. If
F_{\#}^{X}\nu_{r}^{X}
is
1.01
-isotropic
with its mean lying within a ball of radius
0.01
, then
(4F^{X})_{\#}\nu_{\delta r}^{X}

satisfies Assumption
4.4
(i.e., evaluation oracle,
inclusion of
B_{1}(0)
, well-roundedness, and ground set equal to
its support).

Proof.

By setting
U=V\circ(H^{X})^{-1}/\mathds{1}[x\in H^{X}(\mathsf{L}_{g}\cap B_{\delta r}(0))]

with
H^{X}:=4F^{X}
, one can implement the evaluation oracle for
the convex
U
(using the oracle for
V
) and recover
H_{\#}^{X}\nu_{\delta r}^{X}\propto\exp(-U)
.
It also follows from the definition of
U
and
\mathsf{L}_{g}
that

U(x)\leq 10n
on
\operatorname{supp}H_{\#}^{X}\nu_{\delta r}^{X}
.

Let us now examine the inclusion of
B_{1}(0)
in its ground set.
When
F^{X}:x\mapsto Ax-\mu
between
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, let us define an affine
map
F_{0}^{X}:x\mapsto Ax-b
from
\mathbb{R}^{n}
to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
which makes
\nu_{r}^{X}

centered, noting that
\lVert b-\mu\rVert\leq 0.01
. As for the lower regularity,
since
(F_{0}^{X})_{\#}\nu_{r}^{X}
is
1.01
-isotropic with zero
mean, by Lemma
A.10
, its support contains
a ball of radius
(e\sqrt{1.01})^{-1}\geq 0.35
centered at the origin.
Hence, as
\lVert b-\mu\rVert\leq 0.01
,
\operatorname{supp}F_{\#}^{X}\nu_{r}^{X}

contains a ball of radius
0.3
centered at the origin, so does
\operatorname{supp}F_{\#}^{X}\nu_{\delta r}^{X}
.
Therefore, by scaling via
x\mapsto 4x
, we can ensure that
\operatorname{supp}H_{\#}^{X}\nu_{\delta r}^{X}

contains a unit ball
B_{1}(0)
. Due to
\min U\geq 0
and
U(x)\leq 10n

on
\operatorname{supp}H_{\#}^{X}\nu_{\delta r}^{X}
, we clearly have
B_{1}(0)\subset\mathsf{L}_{H_{\#}^{X}\nu_{\delta r}^{X},g}
.

We define an affine map
G_{0}^{X}:x\mapsto Ax-\delta b
between

\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Observe that for a constant
c^{\prime}\geq 2\delta
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{(G_{0}^{X})_{\#}\nu_{\delta r}^{X}}\lVert X\rVert

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}_{(G_{0}^{X})_{\#}\nu_{\delta r}^{X}}(\lVert X\rVert\geq u)\,\mathrm{d}u\leq c^{\prime}\sqrt{n}+\int_{c^{\prime}\sqrt{n}}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(\lVert X\rVert\geq u)\,\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle\leq c^{\prime}\sqrt{n}+\delta\int_{c^{\prime}\delta^{-1}\sqrt{n}}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(\lVert X\rVert\geq\delta u)\,\mathrm{d}u\,.

(4.2)

Let us bound the integrand in terms of
\mathbb{P}_{(F_{0}^{X})_{\#}\nu_{r}^{X}}(\lVert X\rVert\geq u)
.
For the embedded
G:\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\to\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
defined by
G(x,t)=(G_{0}^{X}(x),t)
,
we have

\operatorname{supp}G_{\#}\nu_{\delta r}=G(\mathcal{K}\cap C_{g}\cap C_{\delta r})=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}A\\
&1\end{array}}}\right](\mathcal{K}\cap C_{g}\cap C_{\delta r})-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\delta b\\
0\end{array}}}\right]\,,

where
C_{g}=\mathsf{L}_{g}\times\mathbb{R}
and
C_{\delta r}=B_{\delta r}(0)\times\mathbb{R}
.
As
\mathcal{K}
and
C_{g}
contain the origin, and
\delta>1
, we clearly
have
\mathcal{K}\cap C_{g}\cap C_{\delta r}\subset\delta(\mathcal{K}\cap C_{g}\cap C_{r})
,
and thus

\displaystyle\operatorname{supp}G_{\#}\nu_{\delta r}

\displaystyle\subseteq\delta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}A\\
&1\end{array}}}\right](\mathcal{K}\cap C_{g}\cap C_{r})-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\delta b\\
0\end{array}}}\right]=\delta\Bigl(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}A\\
&1\end{array}}}\right](\mathcal{K}\cap C_{g}\cap C_{r})-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\begin{array}[]{c}b\\
0\end{array}}}\right]\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\delta\operatorname{supp}F_{\#}\nu_{r}\,,

where
F:(x,t)\mapsto(F_{0}^{X}(x),t)
is an affine map between
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
.
Moreover, it follows from
\delta C_{l\sqrt{n}}=C_{l\delta\sqrt{n}}

for any
l>0
that

(\operatorname{supp}G_{\#}\nu_{\delta r})\backslash C_{l\delta\sqrt{n}}\subset[\delta\operatorname{supp}F_{\#}\nu_{r}]\backslash[\delta C_{l\sqrt{n}}]=\delta\,[(\operatorname{supp}F_{\#}\nu_{r})\backslash C_{l\sqrt{n}}]\,.

Integrating
\exp(-nt)
over these regions and using
\inf_{\mathcal{K}}t\geq-11

in
(i)
below,

\displaystyle\int_{(\operatorname{supp}G_{\#}\nu_{\delta r})\backslash C_{l\delta\sqrt{n}}}\exp(-nt)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\delta\,[(\operatorname{supp}F_{\#}\nu_{r})\backslash C_{l\sqrt{n}}]}\exp(-nt)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\delta^{n+1}\int_{(\operatorname{supp}F_{\#}\nu_{r})\backslash C_{l\sqrt{n}}}\exp(-nt-t\sqrt{n})\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\underset{(i)}{\leq}e^{11\sqrt{n}}\delta^{n+1}\int_{(\operatorname{supp}F_{\#}\nu_{r})\backslash C_{l\sqrt{n}}}\exp(-nt)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=e^{11\sqrt{n}}\delta^{n+1}\cdot\mathbb{P}_{F_{\#}\nu_{r}}(\lVert X\rVert\geq l\sqrt{n})\int_{\operatorname{supp}F_{\#}\nu_{r}}\exp(-nt)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\,.

(4.3)

Since
(F_{\#}\nu_{r})^{X}=(F_{0}^{X})_{\#}\nu_{r}^{X}
is an
1.01
-isotropic
centered logconcave distribution, a version of Paouris’ theorem (Lemma
A.8
)
implies that for some universal constant
c>0
and any
l\geq 2
,

\mathbb{P}_{F_{\#}\nu_{r}}(\lVert X\rVert\geq l\sqrt{n})=\mathbb{P}_{(F_{0}^{X})_{\#}\nu_{r}^{X}}(\lVert X\rVert\geq l\sqrt{n})\leq\exp(-cl\sqrt{n})\,.

Dividing both sides of (
4.3
) by
\int_{\operatorname{supp}G_{\#}\nu_{\delta r}}\exp(-nt)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t
,
and substituting this Paouris bound,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{(G_{0}^{X})_{\#}\nu_{\delta r}^{X}}(\lVert X\rVert\geq l\delta\sqrt{n})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{G_{\#}\nu_{\delta r}}(\lVert X\rVert\geq l\delta\sqrt{n})

\displaystyle\leq e^{11\sqrt{n}}\delta^{n+1}\exp(-cl\sqrt{n})\,\frac{\int_{\operatorname{supp}F_{\#}\nu_{r}}\exp(-nt)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t}{\int_{\operatorname{supp}G_{\#}\nu_{\delta r}}\exp(-nt)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t}

\displaystyle\leq e^{1+(12-cl)\sqrt{n}}\,.

Putting this bound back to (
4.2
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{(G_{0}^{X})_{\#}\nu_{\delta r}^{X}}\lVert X\rVert

\displaystyle\leq c^{\prime}\sqrt{n}+\delta\int_{c^{\prime}\delta^{-1}\sqrt{n}}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}_{(G_{0}^{X})_{\#}\nu_{\delta r}^{X}}(\lVert X\rVert\geq\delta u)\,\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle\leq c^{\prime}\sqrt{n}+e^{2+12\sqrt{n}}\int_{c^{\prime}\delta^{-1}\sqrt{n}}^{\infty}e^{-cu}\,\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle=c^{\prime}\sqrt{n}+c^{-1}e^{2+12\sqrt{n}}e^{-cc^{\prime}\sqrt{n}/\delta}\,.

By taking
c^{\prime}
large enough, the second term can be made exponentially
small. It then follows from the reverse Hölder (Lemma
A.9
)
that

\mathbb{E}_{(G_{0}^{X})_{\#}\nu_{\delta r}^{X}}[\lVert X\rVert^{2}]\lesssim(\mathbb{E}_{(G_{0}^{X})_{\#}\nu_{\delta r}^{X}}\lVert X\rVert)^{2}\lesssim n\,.

Thus, for
F^{X}(x)=Ax-\mu=G_{0}^{X}+\delta b-\mu
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{F_{\#}^{X}\nu_{\delta r}^{X}}[\lVert X\rVert^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq 2\,\mathbb{E}_{F_{\#}^{X}\nu_{\delta r}^{X}}[\lVert X-\delta b+\mu\rVert^{2}]+2\,\lVert\delta b-\mu\rVert^{2}=2\,\mathbb{E}_{(G_{0}^{X})_{\#}\nu_{\delta r}^{X}}[\lVert X\rVert^{2}]+2\,\lVert\delta b-\mu\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim n+\frac{\lVert b\rVert^{2}}{n}\lesssim n\,,

where the last inequality follows from Lemma
4.13

below. Therefore,
\mathbb{E}_{H_{\#}^{X}\nu_{\delta r}^{X}}[\lVert X\rVert^{2}]=\mathcal{O}(n)
.
∎

We now present a key geometric property concerning the diameter of
the level set of logconcave distributions. We remark that this properly
generalizes
[
LV07
, Lemma 5.18]
, which says that
if
z
is a maximum point of an isotropic logconcave density, then

\lVert z\rVert\leq n+1
.

Lemma 4.13

(Diameter of level sets)
.

Suppose an
isotropic logconcave density in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is proportional to
e^{-V}
.
Then, for
V_{0}=\min V
and any
c>0
,

\max_{x:V(x)-V_{0}\leq cn}\lVert x\rVert\leq 8e^{c+1}\,n\,.

Proof.

Pick any point
x
with
V(x)\leq V_{0}+cn
. We may assume by rotation
that
x=De_{1}
for
D=\lVert x\rVert>0
. Let us consider the marginal
of the density along the direction
e_{1}
, which is also isotropic
with zero mean. We also consider the
(n-1)
-dimensional slices perpendicular
to
e_{1}
at
0
and at
D/n
, calling them
\mathcal{S}(0)
and
\mathcal{S}(D/n)

respectively. We can define a bijective mapping
f:\mathcal{S}(D/n)\to\mathcal{S}(0)

by
y\mapsto z=x+\frac{y-x}{1-n^{-1}}
; in words,
f(y)
is the
point
z\in\mathcal{S}(0)
such that
y\in\overline{zx}
. Using the convexity
of
V
with endpoints
z
and
x
,

V(y)=V\bigl(\frac{n-1}{n}\,z+\frac{1}{n}\,x\bigr)\leq\frac{n-1}{n}\,V(z)+\frac{1}{n}\,V(x)\leq V(z)+c\,.

It then follows that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}(D/n)}e^{-V(y)}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\geq e^{-c}\int_{\mathcal{S}(D/n)}e^{-V(f(y))}\,\mathrm{d}y=\frac{e^{-c}}{\lvert Df\rvert}\int_{\mathcal{S}(0)}e^{-V(z)}\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle=\bigl(1-\frac{1}{n}\bigr)^{n-1}e^{-c}\int_{\mathcal{S}(0)}e^{-V(z)}\,\mathrm{d}z\geq e^{-c-1}\int_{\mathcal{S}(0)}e^{-V(z)}\,\mathrm{d}z\,.

By Lemma
A.11
, the density of the marginal at
0

is at least
1/8
, so the density at
D/n
is at least
e^{-c-1}/8
.
Since the value of the marginal density function is at least
\min(1/8,e^{-c-1}/8)=e^{-c-1}/8

due to the convexity of
V
, the measure of the marginal in the range

[0,D/n]
is at least

\frac{D}{8n}e^{-c-1}.

As this must be at most
1
(actually at most
1-(1/e)
), we obtain
that
D\leq 8e^{c+1}\,n
as claimed.
∎

4.3
Rounding the grounded distribution

We have justified Step 1 of the overall approach thus far. We now
examine Step 2 and 3.

Step 2 (
\nu_{D}^{X}\to\nu^{X}
).

Given an affine map
F^{X}
such that
F_{\#}^{X}\nu_{D}^{X}
is

1.01
-isotropic, and we show that
(4F^{X})_{\#}\nu^{X}
satisfies
Assumption
4.4
. The following result allows us
to bound the complexity of
\mathsf{Isotropize}
on
(4F^{X})_{\#}\nu^{X}

by
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{3})
as well.

Lemma 4.14
.

Let
F^{X}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an invertible affine
map, and
D=2R\log 400
. If
F_{\#}^{X}\nu_{D}^{X}
is
1.01
-isotropic
with its mean located within
0.01
from the origin, then
(4F^{X})_{\#}\nu_{\delta r}^{X}

satisfies Assumption
4.4
.

Proof.

Since
\nu^{X}
is
1.01
-warm with respect to
\pi^{X}
due to
(
4.1
),
\mathbb{E}_{\nu^{X}}[\lVert X\rVert^{2}]\leq 1.01\,\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{X}}[\lVert X\rVert^{2}]\leq(2R)^{2}
,
so Lemma
A.7
leads to

\mathbb{P}_{\nu^{X}}(\lVert X\rVert\geq 2tR)\leq e^{-t+1}\quad\text{for any }t\geq 1\,.

Thus,
\mathbb{P}_{\nu^{X}}(\lVert X\rVert\leq D)\geq 1-\varepsilon
for
D=2R\log\frac{e}{\varepsilon}
,
which implies

\frac{\nu_{D}^{X}}{\nu^{X}}=\bigl(\mathbb{P}_{\nu^{X}}(\operatorname{supp}\nu_{D}^{X})\bigr)^{-1}=\bigl(\mathbb{P}_{\nu^{X}}(\lVert X\rVert\leq D)\bigr)^{-1}\leq(1-\varepsilon)^{-1}\,.

It means that
\mu_{D}:=F_{\#}^{X}\nu_{D}^{X}
is also
(1-\varepsilon)^{-1}
-warm
with respect to
\mu:=F_{\#}^{X}\nu^{X}
.

We first address the upper regularity
(\mathsf{UR})
. For any unit
vector
v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\displaystyle\bigl|\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{D}}\lvert X\cdot v\rvert-\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\lvert X\cdot v\rvert\bigr|

\displaystyle=\Big|\int\lvert X\cdot v\rvert\,(\mathrm{d}\mu_{D}-\mathrm{d}\mu)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\lvert X\cdot v\rvert^{2}]}\sqrt{\chi^{2}(\mu_{D}\mathbin{\|}\mu)}

\displaystyle\leq 4\,\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\lvert X\cdot v\rvert\,\bigl(\frac{\mu_{D}}{\mu}-1\bigr)\,,

where the last line follows from the reverse Hölder (Lemma
A.9
).
Rearranging terms, we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\lvert X\cdot v\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1-\varepsilon}{1-5\varepsilon}\,\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{D}}\lvert X\cdot v\rvert\leq\frac{1-\varepsilon}{1-5\varepsilon}\,\bigl(\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{D}}\lvert(X-\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{D}}X)\cdot v\rvert+0.01\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1-\varepsilon}{1-5\varepsilon}\times 1.02\,.

Hence, by taking
\varepsilon
small enough, we can ensure that
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\lvert X\cdot v\rvert\leq 1.05

for any
v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and this immediately implies that

\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\lVert X\rVert^{2}]=\mathbb{E}_{F_{\#}^{X}\nu^{X}}[\lVert X\rVert^{2}]=\mathcal{O}(n)\,.

As for the lower regularity
(\mathsf{LR})
, we already showed in the
proof of Lemma
4.12
that due to the isotropy, the
support of
F_{\#}^{X}\nu_{D}^{X}
contains a ball of radius
0.3

centered at the origin, so does that of
F_{\#}^{X}\nu^{X}
. Therefore,
scaling by
x\mapsto 4x
, we can conclude that
B_{1}(0)\subseteq\operatorname{supp}(4F^{X})_{\#}\nu^{X}

and that
\mathbb{E}_{(4F^{X})_{\#}\nu^{X}}[\lVert X\rVert^{2}]=\mathcal{O}(n)
. The implementability
of the evaluation oracle for the potential, as well as
\operatorname{supp}(4F^{X})_{\#}\nu^{X}=\mathsf{L}_{(4F^{X})_{\#}\nu^{X},g}
,
can be proven similarly to Lemma
4.12
.
∎

Step 3 (
\nu^{X}\to\pi^{X}
).

We show that if
F_{\#}^{X}\nu^{X}
is
1.01
-isotropic, then the
largest eigenvalue of the covariance of
(4F^{X})_{\#}\pi^{X}
is

\mathcal{O}(1)
, and its ground set contains
B_{1}(0)
.

Lemma 4.15
.

Let
F^{X}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an invertible affine
map such that
F_{\#}^{X}\nu^{X}
is
1.01
-isotropic with its mean
located within
0.01
from the origin. Then, for the affine map
H^{X}=4F^{X}
,
the ground set
H^{X}\mathsf{L}_{g}
of
H_{\#}^{X}\pi^{X}
contains
a unit ball
B_{1}(0)
, and
\lVert\operatorname{Cov}H_{\#}^{X}\pi^{X}\rVert=\mathcal{O}(1)
.

Proof.

As in the proof of Lemma
4.14
, for any unit vector
v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\bigl|\mathbb{E}_{F_{\#}^{X}\nu^{X}}\lvert X\cdot v\rvert-\mathbb{E}_{F_{\#}^{X}\pi^{X}}\lvert X\cdot v\rvert\bigr|\leq 4\,\mathbb{E}_{F_{\#}^{X}\pi^{X}}\lvert X\cdot v\rvert\,\bigl(\frac{\nu^{X}}{\pi^{X}}-1\bigr)\leq 0.04\,\mathbb{E}_{F_{\#}^{X}\pi^{X}}\lvert X\cdot v\rvert\,,

and thus
\mathbb{E}_{F_{\#}^{X}\pi^{X}}\lvert X\cdot v\rvert\leq 1.05\,\mathbb{E}_{F_{\#}^{X}\nu^{X}}\lvert X\cdot v\rvert
.
Using the reverse Hölder inequality in
(i)
below,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{F_{\#}^{X}\pi^{X}}[\lvert(X-\mathbb{E}_{F_{\#}^{X}\pi^{X}}X)\cdot v\rvert^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{F_{\#}^{X}\pi^{X}}[\lvert X\cdot v\rvert^{2}]\underset{(i)}{\leq}16\,(\mathbb{E}_{F_{\#}^{X}\pi^{X}}\lvert X\cdot v\rvert)^{2}\leq 20\,(\mathbb{E}_{F_{\#}^{X}\nu^{X}}\lvert X\cdot v\rvert)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 20\,\bigl(\mathbb{E}_{F_{\#}^{X}\nu^{X}}\lvert(X-\mathbb{E}_{F_{\#}^{X}\nu^{X}}X)\cdot v\rvert+0.01\bigr)^{2}\leq 50\,.

Thus, the largest eigenvalue of
\operatorname{Cov}H_{\#}^{X}\pi^{X}
is
\mathcal{O}(1)
,
and the ground set of
H_{\#}^{X}\pi^{X}
contains
B_{1}(0)
.
∎

We note that
\mathbb{E}_{H_{\#}^{X}\pi^{X}}[\lVert X\rVert^{2}]=\mathcal{O}(n)
, so we can
first run
\mathsf{Tilted\ Gaussian\ Cooling}
to generate an
\mathcal{O}(1)
-warm start
Z_{0}=(X_{0},T_{0})

for
H_{\#}\pi
, using
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{3})
queries in expectation.
Then, as in
\mathsf{Isotropize}
, we run
\mathsf{PS}_{N}(H_{\#}\pi,\delta_{Z_{0}},h)

with
N\asymp n^{2}\operatorname{polylog}n
and
h\asymp(n^{2}\operatorname{polylog}n)^{-1}
,
obtaining
k\asymp n\operatorname{polylog}n
many samples to compute the empirical
covariance
\overline{\Sigma}
for
\Sigma=\operatorname{Cov}H_{\#}^{X}\pi^{X}
.
It then follows from Lemma
4.6
that with
probability at least
1-\mathcal{O}(n^{-1})
,

0.99\,\overline{\Sigma}\preceq\Sigma\preceq 1.01\,\overline{\Sigma}\,.

Thus,
(\overline{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\circ H^{X})_{\#}\pi^{X}
becomes

1.01
-isotropic. Since one iteration of
\mathsf{PS}_{N}
uses
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(N)

queries, the total complexity of this procedure (in addition to
\mathsf{Tilted\ Gaussian\ Cooling}
)
would be
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{3})
. Therefore, Step 3 also uses
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{3})

queries in expectation.

5
Application 2: Integrating logconcave functions

In this section, we propose a faster algorithm for integrating logconcave
functions, leveraging our improved sampling guarantees and streamlining
previous analysis in
[
KLS97
,
LV06c
,
CV18
]
.

Problem 5.1

(Integration)
.

Assume access to a well-defined
function oracle
\mathsf{Eval}_{x_{0},R}(V)
for convex
V:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}
.
Given
\varepsilon>0
, what is the query complexity of computing a
(1+\varepsilon)
-multiplicative
approximation
F
to the integral of
f=e^{-V}
with probability
at least
3/4
, i.e.,

(1-\varepsilon)\int f\leq F\leq(1+\varepsilon)\int f\,.

We can assume that
\min V=0
and that
\pi^{X}
is well-rounded
due to the rounding algorithm in the previous section. Hereafter,
we assume that
x_{0}=0
,
R^{2}=C^{2}n
for a known constant
C>0
,

\mathcal{K}=\{(x,t):\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}:V(x)-11n\leq nt\}
, and that for
l=C^{2}\vee\log\frac{8e}{\varepsilon}
,

\bar{\mathcal{K}}=\mathcal{K}\cap\{B_{Rl}(0)\times[-11,4+13l]\}\,.

We recall that
\bar{\mathcal{K}}
takes up
(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{4})
measure
of
\pi
and denote
\bar{\pi}:=\pi|_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}
. In this section,
we establish an improved complexity of integrating a logconcave function.

Theorem 5.2

(Restatement of Theorem
1.8
)
.

For any integrable
logconcave
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
given by a well-rounded function
oracle and any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists an algorithm that with
probability at least
3/4
, returns a
(1+\varepsilon)
-multiplicative
approximation to the integral of
f
using
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{3}/\varepsilon^{2})

queries. For an arbitrary logconcave
f
, the total query complexity
is bounded by
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{3.5}\operatorname{polylog}R+n^{3}/\varepsilon^{2})
.

5.1
Algorithm

The main idea for integration is also annealing, and will parallel
the warm-start generation but in a more sophisticated way. We consider
a sequence of distributions in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
starting with a simple
logconcave function that is easy to integrate and ending with the
target logconcave function, where two consecutive distributions
\mu_{i}

and
\mu_{i+1}
satisfy two conditions: (i)
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}(\mu_{i}\mathbin{\|}\mu_{i+1})=\mathcal{O}(1)

for fast sampling from
\mu_{i+1}
started at
\mu_{i}
, and (ii)

\mathcal{R}_{2}(\mu_{i+1}\mathbin{\|}\mu_{i})=\mathcal{O}(n^{-1})
or
\mathcal{O}(\nicefrac{{\sigma^{2}}}{{C^{2}n}})

for small variance of estimators used in the algorithm.

This annealing scheme is used in
[
CV18
]
for the
special case of Gaussians restricted to convex bodies. It needs an
efficient sampler alongside with good dependence on the warm start
and output guarantees. These were available at the time for Gaussians
restricted to convex bodies. Our new sampler allows us to get similar
strong guarantees for arbitrary logconcave distributions, so we will
be able to extend the guarantees to logconcave integration by addressing
additional technical difficulties.

Setup.

Let
m
be the number of samples used for estimation, and
p
the
number of total inner phases. Then, the integration algorithm (Algorithm
6
)
initializes
m
independent starts, generating a
thread
of
samples denoted by
(Z_{i}^{s}=(X_{i}^{s},T_{i}^{s}))_{0\leq i\leq p}

for
s\in[m]
. Here, the initial start
Z_{0}^{s}
is drawn from

\exp(-\frac{n}{2}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2})|_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}
, and subsequent samples

(Z_{i}^{s})_{i\in[p]}
drawn through
\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{ann}}
are appended to the
thread. Clearly, for each
i\in[p]
, the
i
-th samples
Z_{i}^{s}

across the threads are independent and have the same law (i.e.,
Z_{i}^{s_{1}}\perp Z_{i}^{s_{2}}

and
\operatorname{law}Z_{i}^{s_{1}}=\operatorname{law}Z_{i}^{s_{2}}
for any
s_{1}\neq s_{2}\in[m]
).

\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{ann}}
with suitable parameters can ensure that the law of a sample

Z_{i}^{s}
, denoted by
\bar{\mu}_{i}=(\bar{\mu}_{i}^{X},\bar{\mu}_{i}^{T})
,
is close to

\displaystyle\mu_{0}(x,t)

\displaystyle\propto\exp\bigl(-\frac{n}{2}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2}\bigr)\big|_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}=:f_{0}(x,t)\,,

\displaystyle\mu_{i}(x,t)

\displaystyle\propto\exp\bigl(-\frac{1}{2\sigma_{i}^{2}}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2}-\rho_{i}t\bigr)\big|_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}=:f_{i}(x,t)\quad\text{for }i\in[p]\,,

and we denote its integral (i.e., the normalization constant) by
F_{i}:=\int f_{i}
.

Estimation using drawn samples.

Algorithm
6
calls Algorithm
5

for estimating
E_{i}:=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{s=1}^{m}Y_{i}^{s}
, just
the empirical average of
Y_{i}^{s}
defined as

Y_{i}^{s}:=\frac{f_{i+1}(Z_{i}^{s})}{f_{i}(Z_{i}^{s})}\quad\text{for }i=0,\dotsc,p\,,\ \text{with convention }f_{p+1}(z):=\exp(-nt)|_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}\,.

Input:
initial point
Z_{i-1}
,
target density
f_{i}
, upcoming density
f_{i+1}
, target accuracy

\epsilon
.

Output:
estimation
E_{i}

and samples
Z_{i}
approximately close to
\mu_{i+1}\propto f_{i+1}
.

1:

For each
s\in[m]
, draw
Z_{i}^{s}
through
\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{ann}}
started
at
Z_{i-1}^{s}
for target
\mu_{i}\propto f_{i}
with accuracy

\epsilon
.

2:

Estimate

E_{i}=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{s=1}^{m}\frac{f_{i+1}(Z_{i}^{s})}{f_{i}(Z_{i}^{s})}\,.

3:

Return
E_{i}
and
\{Z_{i}^{s}\}_{s\in[m]}
.

Algorithm 5

\mathsf{Samp\text{-}Est}\,(Z_{i-1},f_{i},f_{i+1},\epsilon)

If each sample
Z_{i}
were to follow the
exact distribution

\mu_{i}
rather than the approximate one
\bar{\mu}_{i}
, then

\mathbb{E}E_{i}=\mathbb{E}Y_{i}=\int\frac{f_{i+1}(z)}{f_{i}(z)}\,\frac{f_{i}(z)}{\int f_{i}}\,\mathrm{d}z=\frac{F_{i+1}}{F_{i}}\,,

and thus

\underbrace{\int_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}\exp\bigl(-\frac{n}{2}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2}\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t}_{=:I_{0}}\times\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{0}}Y_{0}\cdots\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{p}}Y_{p}=F_{0}\,\frac{F_{1}}{F_{0}}\cdots\frac{F_{p+1}}{F_{p}}=\int_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}\exp(-nt)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t\,.

Since
\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(X\in\bar{\mathcal{K}})\geq 1-\varepsilon
by construction, and
\int_{\mathcal{K}}\exp(-nt)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t=\frac{1}{n}\int e^{-V}
,
it implies that

(1-\varepsilon)\int e^{-V}\leq nI_{0}\times\mathbb{E}Y_{0}\cdots\mathbb{E}Y_{p}\leq\int e^{-V}\,.

(5.1)

While estimating
\mathbb{E}Y_{i}
is straightforward since
\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{ann}}
yields
as many samples from
\bar{\mu}_{i}\approx\mu_{i}
as needed, we
should also be able to estimate the initial integral
I_{0}=\int_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}\exp(-\frac{n}{2}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2})\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t
.

The initial integral
I_{0}
.

For
I:=[-11,4+13l]
, we define
f_{-1}(x,t):=\exp(-\frac{n}{2}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2})\cdot\mathds{1}_{I}(t)
,
and
\mu_{-1}(x,t)=\mathcal{N}(0,n^{-1}I_{n})\otimes\text{Unif}\,I\propto f_{-1}(x,t)
.
Using the estimate

Y_{-1}(x,t):=\frac{f_{0}(x,t)}{f_{-1}(x,t)}=\frac{\exp(-\frac{n}{2}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2})\cdot\mathds{1}_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}(x,t)}{\exp(-\frac{n}{2}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2})\cdot\mathds{1}_{I}(t)}=\mathds{1}_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}(x,t)\quad\text{for }(x,t)\in\operatorname{supp}\mu_{-1}\,,

and its empirical average
E_{-1}:=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{s=1}^{m}Y_{-1}^{s}
,
we can approximate
I_{0}
through
E_{-1}\int f_{-1}
due to

E_{-1}\int f_{-1}\approx\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{-1}}Y_{-1}\times\int f_{-1}=\int\mathds{1}_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}(x,t)\,f_{-1}(x,t)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t=\int_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}\exp\bigl(-\frac{n}{2}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2}\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t=I_{0}\,.

Here,
\int f_{-1}
can be computed by a formula

\int f_{-1}=\int\exp(-\frac{n}{2}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2})\,\mathrm{d}x\times(15+13l)=(2\pi n^{-1})^{n/2}(15+13l)\,.

Overview of algorithm.

We propose a new annealing scheme (Algorithm
6
)
to fulfill the two conditions on
\mathcal{R}_{2}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}
.

•

[Phase I]
While
\sigma^{2}\in[n^{-1},1]
:

–

End-to-end:
\mu_{\text{start}}\propto\exp(-\frac{n}{2}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2})|_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}

to
\mu_{\text{end}}\propto\exp(-\frac{1}{2}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2})_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}
.

–

Update:
\sigma^{2}\leftarrow\sigma^{2}\,(1+\frac{1}{n})
and
\rho=0
.

–

Guarantee:
\mathcal{R}_{2}(\mu_{i+1}\mathbin{\|}\mu_{i})=\mathcal{O}(n^{-1})
&
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n)

phases (Lemma
5.8
).

•

[Phase II]
While
\rho\leq n
:

–

End-to-end:
\mu_{\text{start}}\propto\exp(-\frac{1}{2}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2}-\frac{1}{28l}\,t)|_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}

to
\mu_{\text{end}}\propto\exp(-\frac{1}{2}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2}-nt)_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}
.

–

Update: [
\sigma^{2}\leftarrow\sigma^{2}/(1+\frac{1}{28nl})
and
\rho\leftarrow\rho\,(1+\frac{1}{28nl})
]
and [
\sigma^{2}\leftarrow\sigma^{2}\,(1+\frac{1}{28nl})
and
\rho\leftarrow\rho
].
4
4

4

In fact, we can combine two updates, but we separate them for ease
of analysis.

–

Guarantee:
\mathcal{R}_{2}(\mu_{i+1}\mathbin{\|}\mu_{i})=\mathcal{O}(n^{-1})
&
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(nl)

phases (Lemma
5.10
).

•

[Phase III]
While
\sigma^{2}\in[1,C^{2}n]
:

–

End-to-end:
\mu_{\text{start}}\propto\exp(-\frac{1}{2}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2}-nt)|_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}

to
\mu_{\text{end}}\propto\exp(-\frac{1}{2C^{2}n}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2}-nt)_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}
.

–

Update
\sigma^{2}\leftarrow\sigma^{2}\,(1+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{C^{2}n})

and
\rho=n
.

–

Guarantee:
\mathcal{R}_{2}(\mu_{i+1}\mathbin{\|}\mu_{i})=\mathcal{O}(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{C^{2}n})

&
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{C^{2}n}{\sigma^{2}})
phases for doubling of
\sigma^{2}

(see Lemma
5.11
).

In summary,
\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{ann}}
started at
\bar{\mu}_{i-1}
generates samples

Z\sim\bar{\mu}_{i}
, and estimate
f_{i+1}(Z)/f_{i}(Z)
. Meanwhile,
we would like to ensure that (1)
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}(\mu_{i-1}\mathbin{\|}\mu_{i})=\mathcal{O}(1)

when moving from
\bar{\mu}_{i-1}
to
\bar{\mu}_{i}
, and (2)
\mathcal{R}_{2}(\mu_{i+1}\mathbin{\|}\mu_{i})=\mathcal{O}(n^{-1})

in Phase I and II, and
\mathcal{O}(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{C^{2}n})
in Phase III
for variance control of
f_{i+1}/f_{i}
.

Input:
a logconcave function

f
in Problem
5.1
, and target accuracy
\varepsilon>0
.

Output:
an estimation
E

for
\int f
.

1:

\diamond

i=0,l=C^{2}\vee\log\frac{8e}{\varepsilon},(\sigma_{0}^{2},\rho_{0})=(\frac{1}{n},0),m=\frac{10^{11}l\log nl\log C^{2}n}{\varepsilon^{2}},\epsilon=\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{10^{15}C^{2}nl^{2}\log^{3}(C^{2}nl)}
.
Denote

f_{j}(x,t):=\exp\bigl(-\frac{1}{2\sigma_{j}^{2}}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2}-\rho_{j}t\bigr)\big|_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}\,.

2:

Get
\{Z_{-1}^{s}\}_{s\in[m]}\sim\mu_{-1}=\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{1}{n}I_{n})\otimes\text{Unif}\,([-11,4+13l])
,
and compute
E_{-1}=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{s=1}^{m}\mathds{1}_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}(Z_{-1}^{s})
.

3:

while

\sigma_{i}^{2}\leq 1

do

4:

If
\sigma_{i}^{2}=1
, then go to next
while
after
this iteration. Set

\begin{cases}\sigma_{i+1}^{2}=1\wedge\sigma_{i}^{2}(1+\frac{1}{n})\quad\&\quad\rho_{i+1}=0&\text{if }\sigma_{i}^{2}<1\,,\\
\sigma_{i+1}^{2}=\sigma_{i}^{2}\quad\&\quad\rho_{i+1}=\frac{1}{28l}&\text{if }\sigma_{i}^{2}=1\,.\end{cases}

5:

Run
(E_{i},\{Z_{i}^{s}\}_{s\in[m]})\leftarrow\mathsf{Samp\text{-}Est}\,(\{Z_{i-1}^{s}\}_{s\in[m]},f_{i},f_{i+1},\epsilon)

and increment
i
.

6:

end

while

7:

while

\rho_{i}<n

do

8:

Set
\beta=1+\frac{1}{28nl}
if
\beta\rho_{i}<n
, and
\beta=n/\rho_{i}

otherwise.

9:

(E_{i},\{Z_{i}^{s}\}_{s\in[m]})\leftarrow\mathsf{Samp\text{-}Est}\,(\{Z_{i-1}^{s}\}_{s\in[m]},f_{i},f_{i+1},\epsilon)

for
\sigma_{i+1}^{2}=\frac{\sigma_{i}^{2}}{\beta},\rho_{i+1}=\beta\rho_{i}
.
Increment
i
.

10:

(E_{i},\{Z_{i}^{s}\}_{s\in[m]})\leftarrow\mathsf{Samp\text{-}Est}\,(\{Z_{i-1}^{s}\}_{s\in[m]},f_{i},f_{i+1},\epsilon)

for
\sigma_{i+1}^{2}=\beta\sigma_{i}^{2},\rho_{i+1}=\rho_{i}
. Increment

i
.

11:

end

while

12:

while

\sigma_{i}^{2}\leq C^{2}n

do

13:

If
\sigma_{i}^{2}=C^{2}n
, then
leave
this loop
after this iteration. Set

\begin{cases}\sigma_{i+1}^{2}=C^{2}n\wedge\sigma_{i}^{2}(1+\frac{\sigma_{i}^{2}}{C^{2}n})\quad\&\quad\rho_{i+1}=n&\text{if }\sigma_{i}^{2}<C^{2}n\,,\\
\sigma_{i+1}^{2}=\infty\quad\&\quad\rho_{i+1}=n&\text{if }\sigma_{i}^{2}=C^{2}n\,.\end{cases}

14:

(E_{i},\{Z_{i}^{s}\}_{s\in[m]})\leftarrow\mathsf{Samp\text{-}Est}\,(\{Z_{i-1}^{s}\}_{s\in[m]},f_{i},f_{i+1},\epsilon)

and increment
i
.

15:

end

while

16:

Return
E=n\,(2\pi n^{-1})^{n/2}(15+13l)\times E_{-1}E_{0}\cdots E_{i}

as the estimate of the integral of
f
.

Algorithm 6

\mathsf{Integral}\,
(
f,\varepsilon
)

5.2
Streamlined analysis

We show that our estimate
E_{-1}\cdots E_{p}
concentrates around

\mathbb{E}Y_{-1}\cdots\mathbb{E}Y_{p}
with probability at least
0.9
(say).

5.2.1
Technical tools

Two sources of complications.

Unfortunately, the samples
Z_{i}
in the algorithm follow an
approximate
distribution

\bar{\mu}_{i}
(not exactly the target
\mu_{i}
),
so this entails a coupling characterization of
\mathsf{TV}
-distance in
prior work. Once this issue is properly addressed, readers may want
to use a concentration of each
E_{i}
around
\mathbb{E}E_{i}
based on

\operatorname{var}E_{i}
, but this does not suit our purpose. For example, in
Phase I, the (relative) variance
\operatorname{var}(E_{i}/\mathbb{E}E_{i})
is bounded
by
1/n
, so this leads to
E_{i}=(1\pm\mathcal{O}(n^{-1/2}))\,\mathbb{E}E_{i}

with high probability. Since there are
p=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n)
many phases,
the relative error of the product of
E_{i}
’s would blow up. Thus,
one should rely on the concentration of the
product

E_{-1}\cdots E_{p}
,
not just combining that of each estimate
E_{i}
. To this end, we
should be able to establish

\operatorname{var}(E_{-1}\cdots E_{p})\lesssim(\mathbb{E}[E_{-1}\cdots E_{p}])^{2}\quad\bigl(\text{equivalently, }\mathbb{E}[E_{-1}^{2}\cdots E_{p}^{2}]=\bigl(1+\mathcal{O}(1)\bigr)\,(\mathbb{E}[E_{-1}\cdots E_{p}])^{2}\bigr)\,.

Unfortunately, another complication comes into play due to
dependence
of samples

within
each thread, so one also needs
\mathbb{E}[E_{-1}\cdots E_{p}]=(1\pm\mathcal{O}(1))\,\mathbb{E}E_{-1}\cdots\mathbb{E}E_{p}
.
Prior analysis of volume algorithms used the notion of
\alpha
-mixing

(called
\mu
-independence therein) to address this technicality.

Definition 5.3

(
\alpha
-mixing,
[
Ros56
]
)
.

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})

be a probability triplet. Given two sub
\sigma
-algebras
\mathcal{A}

and
\mathcal{B}
, their
\alpha
-mixing coefficient is defined by

\alpha(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}):=\sup_{(A,B)\in\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}}\lvert\mathbb{P}(A\cap B)-\mathbb{P}(A)\,\mathbb{P}(B)\rvert\,.

For two random variables
X,Y:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
, its
\alpha
-mixing
coefficient is defined by

\alpha(X,Y)=\sup_{(A,B)\in\sigma(X)\times\sigma(Y)}\lvert\mathbb{P}(X\in A,Y\in B)-\mathbb{P}(X\in A)\,\mathbb{P}(Y\in B)\rvert\,.

This notion naturally arises when bounding the covariance between

X
and
Y
through the following inequality due to
[
Dav68
]
:
for
p,q,r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
with
p^{-1}+q^{-1}+r^{-1}=1
, and
\alpha=\alpha(X,Y)
,

\lvert\operatorname{Cov}(X,Y)\rvert\leq 2\alpha^{1/p}\lVert X\rVert_{q}\lVert Y\rVert_{r}\,.

Previous volume-computation work used this inequality with
p=1

and
q=r=\infty
. Since the estimators
Y_{i}
are unbounded, they
had to introduce several technical tools to handle this issue.

New tools.

We provide new approaches that can handle these technicalities in
a much cleaner way. As for the inexact distribution of samples (i.e.,

\bar{\mu}_{i}
instead of
\mu_{i}
),
\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{ann}}
provides a provable
guarantee on
\lVert\frac{\mathrm{d}\bar{\mu}_{i}}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{i}}-1\rVert_{L^{\infty}}
,
not just
\lVert\bar{\mu}_{i}-\mu_{i}\rVert_{\mathsf{TV}}
, and this allows us
to bypass a coupling argument. As for the dependence of samples, we
use the notion of
\beta
-mixing
(or the
coefficient
of absolute regularity
) instead of
\alpha
-mixing, which is viable
also due to stronger guarantees of our samplers.

Definition 5.4

(
\beta
-mixing,
[
KR60
]
)
.

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})

be a probability triplet. Given two sub
\sigma
-algebras
\mathcal{A}

and
\mathcal{B}
, their
\beta
-mixing coefficient is defined by

\beta(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}):=\lVert\mathbb{P}_{\sigma(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})}-\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{B}}\rVert_{\mathsf{TV}}\,.

For two random variables
X,Y:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
, it has an equivalent
definition of

\beta(X,Y)=\lVert\operatorname{law}(X,Y)-\operatorname{law}X\otimes\operatorname{law}Y\rVert_{\mathsf{TV}}\,.

It holds that
2\alpha\leq\beta
in general (see
[
Dou12
, Proposition 1]
),
so
\beta
-mixing is stronger than
\alpha
-mixing. When
Y
is
generated by taking
k
steps of a Markov chin with kernel
P
started
at
X
, it holds that (see
[
Dav74
, Proposition 1]
)

\beta(X,Y)=\int_{\Omega}\lVert\delta_{x}P^{k}-\operatorname{law}Y\rVert_{\mathsf{TV}}\,(\operatorname{law}X)(\mathrm{d}x)\,.

Recall that when
\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{ann}}
generates
Y\sim\bar{\mu}_{i+1}
from

X\sim\bar{\mu}_{i}
, it follows from Lemma
3.5

that for
k=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{2}(\sigma^{2}\vee l^{2})\operatorname{polylog}\frac{R}{\varepsilon})
,

\sup_{x\in\mathcal{\bar{K}}}\,\lVert\delta_{x}P^{k}-\mu_{i+1}\rVert_{\mathsf{TV}}\leq\varepsilon\,,

and thus

\beta(X,Y)=\int_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}\lVert\delta_{x}P^{k}-\bar{\mu}_{i+1}\rVert_{\mathsf{TV}}\,\mathrm{d}\bar{\mu}_{i}(x)\leq\int_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}\lVert\delta_{x}P^{k}-\mu_{i+1}\rVert_{\mathsf{TV}}\,\mathrm{d}\bar{\mu}_{i}(x)+\lVert\mu_{i+1}-\bar{\mu}_{i+1}\rVert_{\mathsf{TV}}\leq 2\varepsilon\,.

(5.2)

Therefore,
\lVert\operatorname{law}(X,Y)-\operatorname{law}X\otimes\operatorname{law}Y\rVert\leq 2\varepsilon
, and
it implies that we can “decouple”
X
and
Y
(i.e., use
\operatorname{law}X\otimes\operatorname{law}Y=\bar{\mu}_{i}\otimes\bar{\mu}_{i+1}

instead of
\operatorname{law}(X,Y)
) at the cost of small probability
2\varepsilon
.

5.2.2
Sample and query complexities

We now analyze the variance of estimators and query complexities
at initialization, Phase I, Phase II, and Phase III. We begin with
a useful observation.

Lemma 5.5
.

For any
i=-1,0,\dots,p
,

\operatorname{var}_{\mu_{i}}\bigl(\frac{E_{i}}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{i}}E_{i}}\bigr)=\frac{1}{m}\operatorname{var}_{\mu_{i}}\bigl(\frac{Y_{i}}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{i}}Y_{i}}\bigr)=\frac{1}{m}\operatorname{var}_{\mu_{i}}\bigl(\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{i+1}}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{i}}\bigr)=\frac{1}{m}\,\chi^{2}(\mu_{i+1}\mathbin{\|}\mu_{i})\,.

Proof.

The first equality is obvious from independence of samples
Z_{i}^{s}

across threads
s\in[m]
. For the second, due to

\frac{Y_{i}}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{i}}Y_{i}}=\frac{f_{i+1}}{f_{i}}\frac{F_{i}}{F_{i+1}}\,,

and
\mu_{i+1}\ll\mu_{i}
, we clearly have

\operatorname{var}_{\mu_{i}}\bigl(\frac{Y_{i}}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{i}}Y_{i}}\bigr)=\operatorname{var}_{\mu_{i}}\bigl(\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{i+1}}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{i}}\bigr)\,,

and the third one follows from the definition of
\chi^{2}
.
∎

We examine query and sample complexities for bounding the variance
of estimator
E_{i}
by
v
(i.e.,
\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{i}}[E_{i}^{2}]\leq(1+v)\,(\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{i}}E_{i})^{2}
).

Initialization.

At initialization,
\mathsf{Integral}
draws samples from
\mu_{-1}

and
\mu_{0}
in Line
2
through rejection sampling.
Below, we will set the target variance
v_{0}=\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{10^{9}}
,
which requires
m_{0}\geq 10^{11}l/\varepsilon^{2}
.

Lemma 5.6

(Initialization)
.

Given
v>0
, in Line
2
,

\mathsf{Integral}
needs
m\geq\frac{100l}{v}
samples to bound the
relative variance
\operatorname{var}(E/\mathbb{E}E)
by
v
, using
\mathcal{O}(m)
queries
in expectation.

Proof.

For Line
2
, we can show
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}(\mu_{0}\mathbin{\|}\mu_{-1})=\log\mathcal{O}(l)

as in the proof of Lemma
3.12
:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{-1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\exp(-\frac{n}{2}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2})\cdot\mathds{1}_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}(x,t)}{\exp(-\frac{n}{2}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2})\cdot\mathds{1}_{I}(t)}\frac{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\exp(-\frac{n}{2}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2})\,\mathrm{d}x\times\lvert I\rvert}{\int_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}\exp(-\frac{n}{2}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2})\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t}

\displaystyle\leq(15+13l)\,\frac{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\exp(-\frac{n}{2}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2})\,\mathrm{d}x}{\int_{B_{1}^{n}(0)\times[0,1]}\exp(-\frac{n}{2}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2})\,\mathrm{d}x}\leq(15+13l)\,\bigl(\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{N}(0,n^{-1}I_{n})}(\lVert X\rVert\leq 1)\bigr)^{-1}

\displaystyle\lesssim l\,.

Thus, by Lemma
5.5
and
\mathcal{R}_{2}=\log(1+\chi^{2})\leq\mathcal{R}_{\infty}
,

\operatorname{var}_{\mu_{-1}}\bigl(\frac{E_{-1}}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{-1}}E_{-1}}\bigr)=\frac{1}{m}\,\chi^{2}(\mu_{0}\mathbin{\|}\mu_{1})\leq\frac{100l}{m}\leq v\,.

Also, computing
E_{-1}
uses
m
many evaluation queries.
∎

Phase I.

We now analyze the variance of
E_{i}
under the update rule of
\sigma^{2}\leftarrow\sigma^{2}(1+\frac{1}{n})
.
To this end, we recall a useful lemma from
[
KV06
, Lemma 3.2]
:

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\to\mathbb{R}
be an integrable logconcave
function. For
a>0
and
Z(a)=\int f(z)^{a}\,\mathrm{d}z
, the function

a\mapsto a^{n}Z(a)
is logconcave in
a
.

Below, we will set the target variance
v_{1,1}=\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{10^{9}n\log n}

during
\sigma^{2}<1
and
v_{1,2}=\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{10^{9}}
when

\sigma^{2}=1
, each of which requires
m_{1,1}\geq\frac{3\cdot 10^{9}\log n}{\varepsilon^{2}}

and
m_{1,2}\geq\frac{2\cdot 10^{9}}{\varepsilon^{2}}
, respectively.

Lemma 5.8

(Phase I: Line
3
)
.

Given

v>0
and
\epsilon>0
, if
\sigma^{2}<1
, then
\mathsf{Integral}

needs
m\geq\frac{3}{nv}
samples to ensure
\operatorname{var}_{\mu}(E/\mathbb{E}_{\mu}E)\leq v
.
Also, when
\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{ann}}
draws
m
samples with law
\bar{\mu}
satisfying

\mathcal{R}_{\infty}(\bar{\mu}\mathbin{\|}\mu)\leq\epsilon
in each inner phase,
it requires
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(mn^{3}l^{2}\log^{4}\frac{R}{\epsilon})
queries
throughout Phase I. When
\sigma^{2}=1
,
\mathsf{Integral}
needs

m\geq\frac{2}{v}
samples and uses
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(mn^{2}l^{2}\log^{4}\frac{R}{\epsilon})

queries.

Proof.

For
\alpha=1/n
, let us denote

\mu_{i}(x,t)\propto\exp\bigl(-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}/(1+\alpha)}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2}\bigr)\big|_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}\quad\&\quad\mu_{i+1}(x,t)\propto\exp\bigl(-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2}\bigr)\big|_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}\,.

It then follows from the definition of
L^{2}
-norm that

\Bigl\|\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{i+1}}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{i}}\Bigr\|_{L^{2}(\mu_{i})}^{2}=\frac{\int\exp(-\frac{1-\alpha}{2\sigma^{2}}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2})\cdot\mathds{1}_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}\times\int_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}\exp(-\frac{1+\alpha}{2\sigma^{2}}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2})\cdot\mathds{1}_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}}{\bigl(\int_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}\exp(-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2})\cdot\mathds{1}_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}\bigr)^{2}}=\frac{F(1-\alpha)\,F(1+\alpha)}{F(1)^{2}}\,,

where
F(s):=\int\exp(-\frac{s}{2\sigma^{2}}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2})\cdot\mathds{1}_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}
.
Using logconcavity of
s^{n+1}F(s)
in
s
(due to Lemma
5.7
),

\frac{F(1-\alpha)\,F(1+\alpha)}{F(1)^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{(1-\alpha^{2})^{n+1}}\leq 1+\frac{3}{n}\,.

Since
1+\chi^{2}(\mu_{i+1}\mathbin{\|}\mu_{i})=\lVert\mu_{i+1}/\mu_{i}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mu_{i})}^{2}
,
we can conclude that

\operatorname{var}_{\mu_{i}}\bigl(\frac{E_{i}}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{i}}E_{i}}\bigr)=\frac{1}{m}\,\chi^{2}(\mu_{i+1}\mathbin{\|}\mu_{i})\leq\frac{3}{mn}\,.

Hence, it suffices to take
m\geq\frac{3}{nv}
samples for estimation.
Regarding the query complexity, there are
\mathcal{O}(n\log n)
inner phases.
Also,
\mu_{i-1}
is always
\mathcal{O}(1)
-warm with respect to
\mu_{i}
,
as in Lemma
3.13
, thus
\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{ann}}
uses
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{2}l^{2}\log^{4}\frac{R}{\epsilon})

queries in expectation (to obtain one sample which is
\epsilon
-close
to
\mu_{i}
in
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}
). Therefore, Phase I requires
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(mn^{3}l^{2}\log^{4}\frac{R}{\epsilon})

queries.

When
\sigma^{2}=1
, we let
\mu_{i}\propto\exp(-\frac{1}{2}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2})|_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}

and
\mu_{i+1}\propto\exp(-\frac{1}{2}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2}-\rho t)|_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}

for
\rho=\frac{1}{28l}
. Due to

\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{i+1}}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{i}}=\sup_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}\frac{\exp(-\rho t)\int_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}\exp(-\frac{1}{2}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2})}{\int_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}\exp(-\frac{1}{2}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2}-\rho t)}\leq\sup_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}\exp\bigl(\rho\,(t-\min_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}t)\bigr)\leq e\,,

it follows that for
m\geq 2/v
,

\operatorname{var}_{\mu_{i}}\bigl(\frac{E_{i}}{\mathbb{E}E_{i}}\bigr)=\frac{1}{m}\,\chi^{2}(\mu_{i+1}\mathbin{\|}\mu_{i})\leq\frac{2}{m}\leq v\,.

Since
\mu_{i-1}
is
\mathcal{O}(1)
-warm with respect to
\mu_{i}
,
\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{ann}}

uses
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(mn^{2}l^{2}\log^{4}\frac{R}{\epsilon})
queries in
expectation.
∎

Phase II.

Variance control in Phase II requires an additional lemma in
[
CV18
, Lemma 7.8]
.
We present it with a streamlined proof.

Lemma 5.9
.

Suppose a logconcave function
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

has support contained in
B_{R}(0)
. Let
X
be drawn from
\mu_{\sigma^{2}}\propto g(x,\sigma^{2}):=f(x)e^{-\|x\|^{2}/(2\sigma^{2})}

and
Y=g(X,\sigma^{2}(1+\alpha))/g(X,\sigma^{2})
. For
\alpha\leq 1/2
,

\operatorname{var}\bigl(\frac{Y}{\mathbb{E}Y}\bigr)=\chi^{2}(\mu_{\sigma^{2}(1+\alpha)}\mathbin{\|}\mu_{\sigma^{2}})\leq\exp\bigl(\frac{2R^{2}\alpha^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\bigr)-1\,.

Proof.

We can directly show that

\Bigl\|\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{\sigma^{2}(1+\alpha)}}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{\sigma^{2}}}\Bigr\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=\frac{\int f(x)\exp(-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2})\times\int f(x)\exp(-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\frac{1-\alpha}{1+\alpha}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2})}{\bigl(\int f(x)\exp(-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}\,(1+\alpha)}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2})\bigr)^{2}}\,.

Defining
g(s):=\log\bigl(\int f(x)\exp(-\frac{s}{2\sigma^{2}(1+\alpha)}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2})\bigr)
,
we have

\mathcal{R}_{2}(\mu_{\sigma^{2}(1+\alpha)}\mathbin{\|}\mu_{\sigma^{2}})=g(1+\alpha)+g(1-\alpha)-2g(1)=\int_{0}^{\alpha}\bigl(g^{\prime}(1+t)-g^{\prime}(1-t)\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{\alpha}\int_{1-t}^{1+t}g^{\prime\prime}(q)\,\mathrm{d}q\mathrm{d}t\,.

(5.3)

For a logconcave distribution
\nu_{q}(x)\propto f(x)\exp(-\frac{q}{2\sigma^{2}(1+\alpha)}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2})
,
we have

\displaystyle g^{\prime\prime}(q)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}(1+\alpha)}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}q}\Bigl(\frac{\int\lVert x\rVert^{2}f(x)\exp(-\frac{q}{2\sigma^{2}(1+\alpha)}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2})}{\int f(x)\exp(-\frac{q}{2\sigma^{2}(1+\alpha)}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2})}\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\sigma^{4}(1+\alpha)^{2}}\,\Bigl[\frac{\int\lVert x\rVert^{4}f(x)\exp(-\frac{q}{2\sigma^{2}(1+\alpha)}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2})}{\int f(x)\exp(-\frac{q}{2\sigma^{2}(1+\alpha)}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2})}-\Bigl(\frac{\int\lVert x\rVert^{2}f(x)\exp(-\frac{q}{2\sigma^{2}(1+\alpha)}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2})}{\int f(x)\exp(-\frac{q}{2\sigma^{2}(1+\alpha)}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2})}\Bigr)^{2}\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\sigma^{4}(1+\alpha)^{2}}\,\operatorname{var}_{\nu_{q}}(\lVert X\rVert^{2})\underset{\eqref{eq:pi}}{\leq}\frac{1}{q\sigma^{2}(1+\alpha)}\,\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{q}}[\lVert X\rVert^{2}]\,,

where we used
C_{\mathsf{PI}}(\nu_{q})\leq\frac{\sigma^{2}(1+\alpha)}{q}
(Bakry-Émery)
in the last line. Due to
\sup_{\operatorname{supp}\nu_{q}}\lVert X\rVert\leq R
,

g^{\prime\prime}(q)\leq\frac{R^{2}}{q\sigma^{2}}\,.

Putting this bound back to (
5.3
) and integrating,

\mathcal{R}_{2}(\mu_{\sigma^{2}(1+\alpha)}\mathbin{\|}\mu_{\sigma^{2}})\leq\frac{R^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\int_{0}^{\alpha}\int_{1-t}^{1+t}\frac{1}{q}\,\mathrm{d}q\mathrm{d}t=\frac{R^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\int_{0}^{\alpha}\bigl(\log(1+t)-\log(1-t)\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\frac{2R^{2}\alpha^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\,,

which completes the proof.
∎

We now establish sample and query complexities under the two update
rules: for
1\leq\beta\leq 1+\frac{1}{28nl}
, (i)
\sigma^{2}\leftarrow\beta^{-1}\sigma^{2}

and
\rho\leftarrow\beta\rho
, and (ii)
\sigma^{2}\leftarrow\beta\sigma^{2}
.
Below, we will set the target variance
v_{2}=\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2\cdot 10^{9}n\log n}
,
which requires
m_{2}\geq\frac{2\cdot 10^{9}\log nl}{\varepsilon^{2}}
.

Lemma 5.10

(Phase II: Line
7
)
.

Given

v>0
and
\epsilon>0
,
\mathsf{Integral}
needs
m\geq\frac{1}{nlv}

samples to ensure
\operatorname{var}_{\mu}(E/\mathbb{E}_{\mu}E)\leq v
. Also, when
\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{ann}}

draws
m
samples with law
\bar{\mu}
satisfying
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}(\bar{\mu}\mathbin{\|}\mu)\leq\epsilon

in each inner phase, it requires
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(mn^{3}l^{3}\log^{4}\frac{R}{\epsilon})

queries throughout Phase II.

Proof.

In the first update of
\sigma_{i+1}^{2}=\sigma_{i}^{2}/\beta
and

\rho_{i+1}=\beta\rho_{i}
, we can write
\mu_{i}(x,t)\propto\exp(-U(x,t))

and
\mu_{i+1}(x,t)\propto\exp(-\beta U(x,t))
for
U(x,t)=(\frac{1}{2}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2}+\rho t)/\mathds{1}_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}(x,t)
,
where
\frac{1}{28l}\leq\rho<n
. Then,

\Bigl\|\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{i+1}}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{i}}\Bigr\|_{L^{2}(\mu_{i})}^{2}=\frac{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\exp(-(2\beta-1)\,U)\times\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\exp(-U)}{\bigl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\exp(-\beta U)\bigr)^{2}}\,.

Using logconcavity of
s^{n+1}\int\exp(-sU)
in
s
with endpoints

2\beta-1
and
1
(due to Lemma
5.7
),

\frac{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\exp(-(2\beta-1)\,U)\times\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\exp(-U)}{\bigl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\exp(-\beta U)\bigr)^{2}}\leq\frac{\beta^{2n}}{(2\beta-1)^{n}}\leq 1+\frac{1}{nl}\,.

Since
1+\chi^{2}(\mu_{i+1}\mathbin{\|}\mu_{i})=\lVert\mu_{i+1}/\mu_{i}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mu_{i})}^{2}
,
we can conclude that

\operatorname{var}_{\mu_{i}}\bigl(\frac{E_{i}}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{i}}E_{i}}\bigr)=\frac{1}{m}\,\chi^{2}(\mu_{i+1}\mathbin{\|}\mu_{i})\leq\frac{1}{mnl}\,,

so it suffices to take
m\geq\frac{1}{nlv}
samples for estimation.

In the second update of
\sigma_{i+1}^{2}=\beta\sigma_{i}^{2}=1

and
\rho_{i+1}=\rho_{i}
, we can write
\mu_{i}^{X}\propto g(x)\exp(-\frac{\beta}{2}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2})

and
\mu_{i+1}^{X}\propto g(x)\exp(-\frac{1}{2}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2})
for
some logconcave function
g
. Then, for
Y=\frac{\exp(-\lVert x\rVert^{2}/2)}{\exp(-\beta\,\lVert x\rVert^{2}/2)}
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{var}_{\mu_{i}}\bigl(\frac{E_{i}}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{i}}E_{i}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{m}\operatorname{var}_{\mu_{i}}\bigl(\frac{Y}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{i}}Y}\bigr)=\frac{1}{m}\operatorname{var}_{\mu_{i}}\bigl(\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{i+1}^{X}}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{i}^{X}}\bigr)=\frac{1}{m}\operatorname{var}_{\mu_{i}^{X}}\bigl(\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{i+1}^{X}}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{i}^{X}}\bigr)

(5.4)

\displaystyle\underset{(i)}{\leq}\frac{1}{m}\,\Bigl(\exp\bigl(\frac{2C^{2}n\,(\beta-1)^{2}}{1/\beta}\bigr)-1\Bigr)\leq\frac{1}{mnl}\,,

where we used Lemma
5.9
in
(i)
.

Regarding the query complexity, we show that
\mu_{i-1}
is
\mathcal{O}(1)
-warm
with respect to
\mu_{i}
. At the beginning of Phase II, for
t_{0}:=\inf_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}t
,

\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{i-1}}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{i}}=\sup_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}\frac{\exp(-\frac{1}{2}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2})}{\exp(-\frac{1}{2}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2}-\frac{1}{28l}\,t)}\frac{\int_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}\exp(-\frac{1}{2}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2}-\frac{1}{28l}\,t)}{\int_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}\exp(-\frac{1}{2}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2})}\leq\sup_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}\exp\bigl(\frac{1}{28l}\,(t-t_{0})\bigr)\leq e\,.

In the first update,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{i}}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{i+1}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}\frac{\exp(-\frac{1}{2}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2}-\rho t)}{\exp(-\frac{\beta}{2}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2}-\beta\rho t)}\frac{\int_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}\exp(-\frac{\beta}{2}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2}-\beta\rho t)}{\int_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}\exp(-\frac{1}{2}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2}-\rho t)}\leq\sup_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}\frac{\exp\bigl(-\frac{1}{2}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2}-\rho\,(t-t_{0})\bigr)}{\exp\bigl(-\frac{\beta}{2}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2}-\beta\rho\,(t-t_{0})\bigr)}

\displaystyle\leq\sup\exp\bigl(\frac{1}{56nl}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2}+\frac{\rho}{28nl}\,(t-t_{0})\bigr)\leq e^{2}\,.

In the second update, the warmness can be shown as in Lemma
3.13
.
Putting these together,
\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{ann}}
uses
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{2}l^{2}\log^{4}\frac{R}{\epsilon})

queries per inner phase, and there are
\mathcal{O}(nl\log nl)
inner phases
in Phase II, so we need
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(mn^{3}l^{3}\log^{4}\frac{R}{\epsilon})

queries throughout Phase II.
∎

Phase III.

We establish sample and query complexity under the update rule of

\sigma^{2}\leftarrow\sigma^{2}\,(1+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{C^{2}n})
. Since

\sigma^{2}
increases from
1
to
C^{2}n
, we can partition the
whole phase by
\mathcal{O}(\log C^{2}n)
many doubling phases, where an initial

\sigma^{2}
doubles in each doubling phase. Below, we will set the
target variance
v_{3,1}=\frac{\varepsilon^{2}\sigma^{2}}{10^{9}C^{2}n\log C^{2}n}

during each doubling phase, where
\sigma^{2}
is an initial value
in the doubling phase. This requires
m_{3,1}\geq\frac{4\cdot 10^{9}\log C^{2}n}{\varepsilon^{2}}
.
When
\sigma^{2}=C^{2}n
, we will set
v_{3,2}=\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{10^{9}}
,
which require
m_{3,2}\geq\frac{2\cdot 10^{9}}{\varepsilon^{2}}
.

Lemma 5.11

(Phase III: Line
12
)
.

Given

v>0
and
\epsilon>0
,
\mathsf{Integral}
needs
m\geq\frac{4\sigma^{2}}{C^{2}nv}

samples to ensure
\operatorname{var}_{\mu}(E/\mathbb{E}_{\mu}E)\leq v
. Also, when
\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{ann}}

draws
m
samples with law
\bar{\mu}
satisfying
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}(\bar{\mu}\mathbin{\|}\mu)\leq\epsilon

in each inner phase, it requires
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(mC^{2}n^{3}l^{2}\log^{4}\frac{R}{\epsilon})

queries during doubling of
\sigma^{2}
. When
\sigma^{2}=C^{2}n
,

\mathsf{Integral}
needs
m\geq\frac{2}{v}
samples and uses
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(mn^{2}l^{2}\log^{4}\frac{R}{\epsilon})

queries.

Proof.

Regarding the variance control, similar to (
5.4
),
we can use Lemma
5.9
(with
R=C\sqrt{n}
and

\alpha=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{C^{2}n}
) to show that for
\sigma^{2}\in[1,C^{2}n]
,

\operatorname{var}_{\mu_{i}}\bigl(\frac{E_{i}}{\mathbb{E}E_{i}}\bigr)=\frac{1}{m}\operatorname{var}\bigl(\frac{Y}{\mathbb{E}Y}\bigr)\leq\frac{1}{m}\,\Bigl(\exp\bigl(\frac{2C^{2}n\alpha^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\bigr)-1\Bigr)\leq\frac{4\sigma^{2}}{C^{2}mn}\,.

Hence, it suffices to take
m\geq\frac{4\sigma^{2}}{C^{2}nv}
samples
for estimation. Next, we can show that
\mu_{i-1}
is
\mathcal{O}(1)
-warm
with respect to
\mu_{i}
during Phase III, as in the proof of Lemma
3.14
.
Since there are
\mathcal{O}(\frac{C^{2}n}{\sigma^{2}})
inner phases (for
doubling of
\sigma^{2}
), and
\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{ann}}
requires
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{2}\sigma^{2}l^{2}\log^{4}\frac{R}{\epsilon})

queries, Phase III uses
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(mC^{2}n^{3}l^{2}\log^{4}\frac{R}{\epsilon})

queries in expectation during each doubling phase.

When
\sigma^{2}=C^{2}n
, we let
\mu_{i}\propto\exp(-\frac{1}{2C^{2}n}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2}-nt)|_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}

and
\mu_{i+1}=\bar{\pi}\propto\exp(-nt)|_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}
. Due to

\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{i+1}}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{i}}=\sup_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}\frac{\exp(\frac{1}{2C^{2}n}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2})\int_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}\exp(-\frac{1}{2C^{2}n}\,\lVert x\rVert^{2}-nt)}{\int_{\bar{\mathcal{K}}}\exp(-nt)}\leq\sqrt{e},

it follows that for
m\geq 2/v
,

\operatorname{var}_{\mu_{i}}\bigl(\frac{E_{i}}{\mathbb{E}E_{i}}\bigr)=\frac{1}{m}\,\chi^{2}(\mu_{i+1}\mathbin{\|}\mu_{i})\leq\frac{2}{m}\leq v\,,

and to sample from
\mu_{i}
,
\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{ann}}
uses
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(mn^{2}l^{2}\log^{4}\frac{R}{\epsilon})

queries in expectation.
∎

5.2.3
Final guarantee

With these bounds on the variance of estimators in
\mathsf{Integral}
,
we can bound the overall variance of the product of estimates using

m
samples. We then show that our final estimate in
\mathsf{Integral}

is close to the integral
\int e^{-V}
with high probability, streamlining
previous analysis in
[
KLS97
,
LV06c
,
CV18
]
.

Lemma 5.12
.

If
\mathsf{Integral}
runs with sample
size
m=\varepsilon^{-2}(10^{11}l\log nl\log C^{2}n)
and sample accuracy

\epsilon\leq\varepsilon^{2}(10^{15}C^{2}nl^{2}\log^{3}(C^{2}nl))^{-1}
,
then it can compute a
(1+\varepsilon)
-multiplicative approximation to

\int e^{-V}
with probability at least
0.98
.

Proof.

We note that the given sample size
m
satisfies all the required
conditions (i.e.,
m\geq m_{0},m_{1,*},m_{2},m_{3,*}
) to attain
a desired target variance in each phase.

We first
assume
that all samples
\{Z_{i}^{s}\}_{0\leq i\leq p,s\in[m]}

for estimations are independent (so
\{E_{i}\}_{i}
are independent)
and exactly distributed according to
\mu_{i}
(not
\bar{\mu}_{i}
).
Then, for
E=E_{-1}E_{0}\cdots E_{p}
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{var}E

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[E_{-1}^{2}]\cdots\mathbb{E}[E_{p}^{2}]-(\mathbb{E}E_{-1})^{2}\cdots(\mathbb{E}E_{p})^{2}=\prod_{i}\bigl(\operatorname{var}E_{i}+(\mathbb{E}E_{i})^{2}\bigr)-\prod_{i}(\mathbb{E}E_{i})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{i}(1+v_{i})\,(\mathbb{E}E_{i})^{2}-\prod_{i}(\mathbb{E}E_{i})^{2}=\Bigl(\prod_{i}(1+v_{i})-1\Bigr)\prod_{i}(\mathbb{E}E_{i})^{2}\leq\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{10^{8}}\prod_{i}(\mathbb{E}E_{i})^{2}\,,

where the last line follows from

\displaystyle\prod_{i}(1+v_{i})

\displaystyle\leq\exp\Bigl(v_{0}+v_{1,1}\times n\log n+v_{1,2}+v_{2}\times 2nl\log nl+\bigl(v_{3,1}\times\frac{C^{2}n}{\sigma^{2}}\bigr)\log C^{2}n+v_{3,2}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\bigl(\frac{6\varepsilon^{2}}{10^{9}}\bigr)\leq 1+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{10^{8}}\,.

By Chebyshev’s inequality,

\mathbb{P}_{(\otimes_{i}\mu_{i})^{\otimes m}}\bigl(\lvert E-\mathbb{E}E\rvert\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{10^{3}}\,\mathbb{E}E\bigr)\leq 0.01\,.

Hence, (
5.1
) implies that with probability
at least
0.99
,

(1-\varepsilon)\int f\leq E=n\,(2\pi n^{-1})^{n/2}(15+13l)\times E_{-1}E_{0}\cdots E_{i}\leq\bigl(1+\frac{\varepsilon}{10^{3}}\bigr)\int f\,.

(5.5)

We now take into two sources of complications: (1) inexact distributions
of samples, and (2) dependence of samples within each thread. As for
(1), since
\mathsf{PS}_{\textup{ann}}
ensures
\frac{\bar{\mu}_{i}}{\mu_{i}}\leq 1+\epsilon
,
we have

\frac{\operatorname{law}Z_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\operatorname{law}Z_{p}}{\mu_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mu_{p}}=\bigotimes_{i}\frac{\bar{\mu}_{i}}{\mu_{i}}\leq e^{2p\epsilon}\,,

and thus

\mathbb{P}_{(\otimes\bar{\mu}_{i})^{\otimes m}}\bigl(\lvert E-\mathbb{E}E\rvert\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{10^{3}}\,\mathbb{E}E\bigr)\leq e^{2mp\epsilon}\,\mathbb{P}_{\otimes\mu_{i}}\bigl(\lvert E-\mathbb{E}E\rvert\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{10^{3}}\,\mathbb{E}E\bigr)\leq 10^{-2}e^{2mp\epsilon}\,.

Regarding (2), the triangle inequality results in

\displaystyle\Bigl\|\operatorname{law}(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{p})-\bigotimes_{i=1}^{p}\operatorname{law}Z_{i}\Bigr\|_{\mathsf{TV}}\leq

\displaystyle\lVert\operatorname{law}(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{p})-\operatorname{law}(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{p-1})\otimes\operatorname{law}Z_{p}\rVert_{\mathsf{TV}}+\Bigl\|\operatorname{law}(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{p-1})\otimes\operatorname{law}Z_{p}-\bigotimes_{i=1}^{p}\operatorname{law}Z_{i}\Bigr\|_{\mathsf{TV}}\,.

The first term equals
\lVert\operatorname{law}(Z_{p-1},Z_{p})-\operatorname{law}Z_{p-1}\otimes\operatorname{law}Z_{p}\rVert_{\mathsf{TV}}
,
as
\{Z_{i}\}_{i}
are generated by a Markov chain (see Lemma
A.4

for details). Applying the DPI to the second term, we obtain that

\displaystyle\Bigl\|\operatorname{law}(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{p})-\bigotimes_{i=1}^{p}\operatorname{law}Z_{i}\Bigr\|_{\mathsf{TV}}

\displaystyle=\lVert\operatorname{law}(Z_{p-1},Z_{p})-\operatorname{law}Z_{p-1}\otimes\operatorname{law}Z_{p}\rVert_{\mathsf{TV}}+\Bigl\|\operatorname{law}(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{p-1})-\bigotimes_{i=1}^{p-1}\operatorname{law}Z_{i}\Bigr\|_{\mathsf{TV}}\,.

As elaborated in (
5.2
), the first term is bounded
by
\epsilon
. Repeating the same argument on the second term, we
have

\Bigl\|\operatorname{law}(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{p})-\bigotimes_{i=1}^{p}\operatorname{law}Z_{i}\Bigr\|_{\mathsf{TV}}\leq 2p\epsilon\,.

Using a similar argument based on the triangle inequality (see Lemma
A.4

for details),

\Bigl\|\operatorname{law}(\{Z_{i}^{s}\}_{i,s})-\Bigl(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{p}\operatorname{law}Z_{i}\Bigr)^{\otimes m}\Bigr\|_{\mathsf{TV}}=\Bigl\|\bigl(\operatorname{law}(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{p})\bigr)^{\otimes m}-\Bigl(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{p}\operatorname{law}Z_{i}\Bigr)^{\otimes m}\Bigr\|_{\mathsf{TV}}\leq 2mp\epsilon\,.

Therefore, it holds from the definition of
\mathsf{TV}
-distance that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{(\operatorname{law}(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{p}))^{\otimes m}}\bigl(\lvert E-\mathbb{E}E\rvert\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{10^{3}}\,\mathbb{E}E\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{(\otimes_{i}\bar{\mu}_{i})^{\otimes m}}\bigl(\lvert E-\mathbb{E}E\rvert\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{10^{3}}\,\mathbb{E}E\bigr)+2mp\epsilon

\displaystyle\leq 10^{-2}e^{2mp\epsilon}+2mp\epsilon\,,

and this is bounded by
0.02
when
\epsilon=(300mp)^{-1}\leq\varepsilon^{2}(10^{15}C^{2}nl^{2}\log^{3}(C^{2}nl))^{-1}
.
Therefore, (
5.5
) holds with probability at least

0.98
.
∎

We are now ready to prove the main result of this section, as claimed
in Theorem
1.8
.

Theorem 5.13
.

In the setting of Problem
5.1
,
for any integrable logconcave
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
given by a
well-rounded function oracle and any
\varepsilon>0
, with probability
at least
3/4
,
\mathsf{Integral}
(Algorithm
6
)
returns a
(1+\varepsilon)
-multiplicative approximation to the integral
of
f
using
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{3}/\varepsilon^{2})
queries. For an arbitrary
logconcave
f
, the total query complexity is bounded by
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{3.5}\operatorname{polylog}R+n^{3}/\varepsilon^{2})
.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.12
, we must have the sample
size
m=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{l\log n}{\varepsilon^{2}})
and sample accuracy
\epsilon=\mathcal{O}(\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{C^{2}nl^{2}\log^{3}(C^{2}nl)})
.
These conditions will decide query and sample complexities in each
phase. At initialization (Lemma
5.6
), under the
choices of
m
and
\epsilon
, we need

\text{Query: }\mathcal{O}(m)=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\bigl(\frac{l\log n}{\varepsilon^{2}}\bigr)\,.

In Phase I (Lemma
5.8
), we need

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}<1:

\displaystyle\text{Query: }\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(mn^{3}l^{2}\log^{4}\frac{R}{\epsilon})=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\bigl(\frac{n^{3}l^{4}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\bigr)\,.

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}=1:

\displaystyle\text{Query: }\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(mn^{2}l^{2}\log^{4}\frac{R}{\epsilon})=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\bigl(\frac{n^{2}l^{3}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\bigr)\,.

In Phase II (Lemma
5.10
), we need

\text{Query: }\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(mn^{3}l^{3}\log^{4}\frac{R}{\epsilon})=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\bigl(\frac{n^{3}l^{4}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\bigr)\,.

In Phase III (Lemma
5.11
), we need

\text{Query: }\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(mC^{2}n^{3}l^{2}\log^{4}\frac{R}{\epsilon}\times\log C^{2}n)=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\bigl(\frac{C^{2}n^{3}l^{3}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\bigr)\,.

Putting these together with
l=C^{2}\vee\log\frac{8e}{\varepsilon}
, the
query complexity of the algorithm is

\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{C^{5}n^{3}}{\varepsilon^{2}})\,.

For general logconcave
f
, we first use the rounding algorithm to
bring
f
to well-rounded position, which takes
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{3.5})

queries and then apply the above for well-rounded
f
.
∎
